## Summary (read this first)

This document describes a self-hosted, always-on AI agent system built to give a solo business owner (triathlon/running coaching business) persistent AI access to his business knowledge base — reachable from a phone, a browser, or a desktop app, without depending on a personal computer being powered on.

**What was built:** A single [Hermes Agent](https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent) instance (Nous Research's open-source, self-improving AI agent framework) deployed via Docker on a self-managed Linux VPS. The agent is powered by Google's Gemini API and exposed through three interfaces — Telegram, a web dashboard, and a native desktop app — all reading and writing to the same underlying agent state, so there is one consistent "brain" regardless of entry point.

**Key technical decisions and why:**

| Decision | Reasoning |
|---|---|
| Self-managed VPS over a managed/turnkey product | The managed product tested first ("Managed Hermes") blocked external connections and general-purpose use (no root access, no exposed ports, no ability to run other services). A plain VPS was required to support the current setup plus planned future services (website hosting, workflow automation). |
| Docker deployment over native install | Isolates the agent process from the host OS; matches the vendor's own recommended production deployment path; simplifies updates (pull new image, recreate container). |
| Tailscale VPN + username/password auth over public OAuth | The dashboard needed to be reachable from a personal laptop without exposing it to the open internet. Public OAuth (Nous Portal) requires an HTTPS callback URL, which would have required a domain name and a reverse proxy with a TLS certificate — real infrastructure overhead for a single-user system. A private VPN (Tailscale) between the two devices removes the need for public exposure entirely, at which point simple username/password auth is appropriate and sufficient. |
| Gemini API, prepaid billing, no auto-reload | Cost control was a stated priority. Prepaid billing with auto-reload disabled means the system fails safe (stops working) rather than accumulating unexpected charges if usage spikes. |
| Local terminal execution backend (not Docker-in-Docker sandboxing) | The agent already runs inside its own Docker container (isolated from the host). Adding a second, nested Docker sandbox layer would have required mounting the host's Docker socket into the container — a well-known technique that effectively grants the container root-equivalent access to the host, which undermines the isolation it's meant to add. The existing outer-container boundary was judged sufficient for a single-operator system. |
| Computer Use tool disabled | The agent runs on a headless server with no desktop environment — this capability had no legitimate use case here and was disabled to reduce attack surface. |
| Telegram allowlist restricted to one user ID | Without this, anyone who discovered the bot's public username could message it and get full access to the agent (terminal, files, business data). |

## Architecture

```mermaid
flowchart TB
    subgraph Devices["Iván's Devices"]
        Phone["📱 Telegram (any device)"]
        Mac["💻 MacBook — Hermes Desktop App"]
        Browser["🌐 Any browser on the Tailscale network"]
    end

    subgraph VPN["Tailscale (private VPN mesh)"]
        TS["Encrypted tunnel — no public exposure required"]
    end

    subgraph VPS["Hostinger VPS — Ubuntu 24.04, KVM2 (2 vCPU / 8GB RAM)"]
        Docker["Docker Engine"]
        subgraph Containers["Docker Containers"]
            GW["hermes-gateway\n(Telegram bot process)"]
            DASH["hermes-dashboard\n(Web UI + REST API, port 9119)"]
        end
        Data["~/.hermes → mounted volume\nconfig.yaml, .env, sessions/, cron/, logs/"]
        GW --- Data
        DASH --- Data
    end

    subgraph External["External Services"]
        Gemini["Google AI Studio\nGemini API (gemini-3.7-flash)"]
        Nous["Nous Research\n(agent framework + optional OAuth)"]
    end

    Phone <--> GW
    Mac <--> TS <--> DASH
    Browser <--> TS
    GW --> Gemini
    DASH --> Gemini
    GW -.skills/updates.-> Nous
```

**In plain language:** there is one program (Hermes Agent) running on a rented server. It's split into two always-on processes — one listens for Telegram messages, the other serves a web dashboard. Both read the same configuration and memory files, and both call out to Google's Gemini model to actually "think." A private VPN (Tailscale) lets the MacBook and any browser on that network reach the dashboard securely, without putting it on the open internet.

---

## Components in detail

### 1. Compute — Hostinger VPS

- Plan: KVM2 (2 vCPU, 8GB RAM), plain Ubuntu 24.04, no pre-installed application template.
- Public IPv4: `179.197.76.70` (SSH/root access only; the dashboard's public binding is not used for login — see Security section).
- Chosen deliberately over Hostinger's packaged "Managed Hermes" and "Hermes Agent" marketplace products, both of which run in a locked-down environment with no root access, no exposed ports, and no way to install additional services. This VPS is a general-purpose box that can also host a website and run workflow automation (n8n) later, which the managed products could not.

### 2. Container runtime — Docker

Installed via Docker's official convenience script (`get.docker.com`). Two long-running containers, both configured with `--restart unless-stopped` so they survive reboots without manual intervention:

| Container | Image command | Purpose | Port |
|---|---|---|---|
| `hermes-gateway` | `nousresearch/hermes-agent gateway run` | Connects to Telegram, handles all bot messages | — (outbound only) |
| `hermes-dashboard` | `nousresearch/hermes-agent dashboard --host 0.0.0.0 --port 9119` | Web UI, REST API, session history, config management | 9119 |

Both containers mount the same host directory (`~/.hermes` → `/opt/data`), which is where all persistent state lives: configuration (`config.yaml`), secrets (`.env`), session transcripts, cron job definitions, and logs. Because both containers share this volume, they operate as one logical agent rather than two separate ones.

### 3. Model provider — Google Gemini (AI Studio)

- Model: `gemini-3.7-flash` — chosen for cost efficiency (flash-tier) and version stability (pinned GA release, not a "-preview" or "-latest" rolling alias that could change behavior unannounced).
  *Updated August 17, 2026 — was `gemini-3.5-flash` from setup until this date. Both criteria that picked 3.5 point harder at 3.7: it is GA (so the pinned-release rule is satisfied at least as well), and it is cheaper on both sides — $0.75/$3.75 per 1M through Dec 31 2026, then $1.50/$7.50, against 3.5-flash's flat $1.50/$9.00. **The reason this is an upgrade and not just a version bump is lesson 7 below**: Hermes's documented failure mode is sustained agentic work, and agentic reliability is precisely what 3.7 was built and benchmarked for (DeepSWE v1.1 65.3% vs 49.0% on 3.6-flash). Full rationale and the models it was chosen over: §33.*
- Billing: Paid tier ("Prepay" billing plan), $30 in prepaid credit, **auto-reload ON at a $7 floor with a $30 top-up**.
  *Corrected August 17, 2026 — this line read "**auto-reload deliberately disabled**. When the balance hits $0, the service pauses rather than charging further — a conscious trade-off favoring predictable cost over uninterrupted uptime." That stopped being true on August 10, 2026, when Iván turned auto-reload on (recorded in `open-loops-archive.md` and nowhere else). Two copies of the old claim survived seven days — this one and `infrastructure.html` §3, both corrected today. **Worth stating why it was not a harmless staleness:** the retired sentence described a deliberate fail-closed design, so anyone reading it would have treated a silent content-engine outage as expected behaviour on a $0 balance rather than as a bug to investigate.*
- Configured via "Full setup" during the Hermes setup wizard (rather than the "Quick Setup / Nous Portal" default) specifically to keep billing on the personally-funded Gemini key rather than a separate Nous Portal subscription.

### 4. Interfaces

**Telegram** — Primary day-to-day interface. A dedicated bot (created via BotFather) connects through the `hermes-gateway` container. Access is restricted via an `allowed_user_ids` allowlist (Telegram numeric user ID only) — an unrestricted bot would be discoverable and usable by anyone who found its username. The user's own Telegram DM is also configured as the "home channel," meaning scheduled/cron job output and cross-platform notifications land there by default.

**Web Dashboard** (`http://100.70.89.17:9119`, Tailscale address only) — Full browser-based admin interface: chat (embeds the same TUI used in the terminal), session history and search, live config editing, API key management, usage/cost analytics, cron job management, and log viewing.

**Hermes Desktop App** (macOS, native) — Configured under Settings → Gateway → Remote Gateway to connect to the same Tailscale address as the dashboard, rather than running its own separate local agent. This was the trickiest piece to get right (see "Notable engineering problems solved" below) and is what unifies all three interfaces into one shared brain, memory, and session history.

### 5. Networking & access control

- **Tailscale**: a WireGuard-based private mesh VPN. Both the VPS and the MacBook are joined to the same private "tailnet." The VPS gets a stable private address (`100.70.89.17`) reachable only by devices on that tailnet — not from the public internet.
- **Dashboard authentication**: two auth providers are registered and stacked (the login page offers both):
  - **Username/password** (`HERMES_DASHBOARD_BASIC_AUTH_*` environment variables, stored in `~/.hermes/.env`, file permissions `600`) — the provider actually in use, appropriate because access is already restricted to the private Tailscale network.
  - **Nous Portal OAuth** — registered (has a valid `agent:...` client ID) but **not currently usable from the public IP**, because Nous Portal requires an HTTPS callback URL for any non-localhost redirect, which this deployment doesn't have (no domain name or reverse proxy configured). Left in place as a future option if a domain + TLS is added later.
- **Terminal execution backend**: set to `local` (executes inside the already-isolated `hermes-gateway`/CLI container itself, not on the bare VPS host, and not in a second nested Docker sandbox).

### 6. Tooling configuration

Default agentic toolset enabled (web search, file operations, code execution, browser automation, memory, cron, task delegation, etc.), with two deliberate deviations from defaults:

- **Computer Use (desktop control) — disabled.** No desktop environment exists on this headless server; the capability had no use and was removed to reduce attack surface.
- **Free/no-key providers selected wherever available**, to avoid unnecessary new accounts and recurring costs: local headless Chromium for browser automation, DuckDuckGo for web search, Microsoft Edge TTS for speech. Image generation was left unconfigured (skipped) — every available provider was paid or subscription-based, and it isn't needed yet.

## Notable engineering problems solved

*Full narratives moved to `build-log.md` (July 29, 2026) — this doc keeps the one-line index so cross-references by number still resolve. Standing rules that came out of these live on here and in the runbooks.*

1. **Split-brain agent** — Desktop App ran its own local agent instead of connecting to the VPS; fixed via Remote Gateway setting.
2. **Managed hosting too restrictive** — "Managed Hermes" blocked external connections entirely; replaced with a plain self-managed VPS.
3. **Docker networking** — app bound to `127.0.0.1` inside the container, unreachable despite `-p` mapping; fixed with `--host 0.0.0.0`.
4. **OAuth redirect mismatch** — Nous Portal needed an HTTPS callback; solved with Tailscale + basic auth instead of a domain/TLS just for login.
5. **Docker-in-Docker sandbox failure** — would have required mounting the host Docker socket (root-equivalent); used `local` backend instead.
6. **Credential hygiene** — a PAT briefly committed publicly, auto-revoked by GitHub. Standing rule: secrets only ever into secure prompts/`.env`/masked fields. Bitwarden adopted July 22, 2026 as the durable vault; SSH keys replaced password login. Open follow-ups: Bitwarden master password out of Apple Passwords, Bitwarden 2FA/Emergency Access, hPanel 2FA.
7. **Hermes unreliability on sustained agentic work (flash-tier)** — narrated success while producing broken code. Standing rules: verify Hermes's claims against literal output; automation logic is written once by hand as a tested script, Hermes only *calls* it. (Twenty API key rotated July 22 as a precaution.)
8. **Google Cloud project silently closed over negligible billing** — abuse-detection false positive; fixed by linking a billing account; 20 days of pixel data lost. Lesson: check request logs before believing account-status banners.

## Current status

Working end-to-end: Telegram bot responds using the VPS-hosted agent; the same agent is reachable via the web dashboard and the Mac Desktop App over Tailscale; all three interfaces share the same configuration, model, and memory. Model confirmed live and responding correctly (`gemini-3.5-flash`). Terminal/file tools confirmed functional via a live test (directory listing).

*Note, August 17, 2026 — the model named in the verification above is the one that was live at the time of that test and is left as the record of it. Hermes now runs `gemini-3.7-flash`; see §3 and §33.*

The business knowledge base (GitHub repo `triaperformance-docs` — pricing, methodology, business overview, growth roadmap, social proof) is cloned onto the VPS at `/opt/data/triaperformance-docs` (host path `~/.hermes/triaperformance-docs`), auto-updated daily via a system cron job (`git pull` at 6am server time), and the agent has been instructed and has persisted to memory that this is its source of truth for business questions. Verified live: asked to summarize the business, the agent correctly read all five documents and returned accurate, correctly-cited figures (revenue by channel, pricing policy, roadmap priorities).

The dashboard's port 9119 is now bound only to the Tailscale interface at the Docker level (`-p 100.70.89.17:9119:9119`, not `0.0.0.0`) — confirmed the public IP no longer connects at all, while the Tailscale address still works. This was necessary because Docker manipulates iptables directly and bypasses `ufw` rules for published container ports; binding to a specific host IP was the reliable fix (a `ufw allow`/`deny` rule on its own does not work against Docker-published ports).

As of July 17, 2026, this box also runs the CoachMatch lead pipeline end to end — n8n handles lead creation and the 3-email nurture sequence, Twenty CRM is the system of record, and Hermes runs a daily WhatsApp follow-up watchdog plus takes free-text status updates over Telegram (see section 8). Verified live with real leads: emails sending, deep links working, and a real Telegram message ("mark Aimee as lost, too expensive") correctly updating Twenty.

## 7. Website hosting — live as of July 16, 2026

`triaperformance.com` is served from the same VPS as Hermes, via native Caddy (not Docker — avoids the iptables/`ufw` interaction documented in problem #3 below, and Caddy's automatic Let's Encrypt HTTPS needed no manual cert work).

- **Webroot**: `/var/www/triaperformance` — deliberately outside `~/.hermes`, so a webserver misconfiguration can't expose Hermes's `.env` or session data.
- **Caddyfile** (`/etc/caddy/Caddyfile`): serves `triaperformance.com` and `www.triaperformance.com` as static files from the webroot, gzip enabled.
- **Source of truth**: the `website/` folder in the `triaperformance-docs` GitHub repo (same repo as the business knowledge base).
- **Deploy script**: `~/.hermes/deploy-website.sh` — pulls the repo and rsyncs `website/` into the webroot. Runs automatically via the existing 6am cron job (which now does knowledge-base sync *and* site deploy in one pass), or on demand via SSH or by asking Hermes to run it.
- **DNS (GoDaddy)**: root `@` and `www` A records now point to the VPS's public IP (`179.197.76.70`), replacing the old HubSpot-hosted site. Mail-related records (MX, SPF, DKIM, DMARC) were left untouched — those are what route `@triaperformance.com` mail through Google Workspace, and are unaffected by hosting changes.
- **Verified live**: `curl -I https://triaperformance.com` returns `HTTP/2 200` via Caddy with a valid TLS cert.

Guardrail carried over from the roadmap: Hermes's command-approval mode should stay on "ask," not "off," for anything that touches `/var/www/triaperformance` or the Caddy config directly — routine content updates go through the git deploy path above, not ad hoc edits.

## 8. Business process automation — CoachMatch lead pipeline (live as of July 17, 2026)

The full lead-to-follow-up pipeline for CoachMatch (TrainingPeaks' coach-matching product) has been rebuilt off HubSpot, split across two systems on purpose: **n8n** for anything deterministic and mechanical (parsing a fixed-template email, calling an API, sending a templated message), and **Hermes** for anything that needs judgment or free-text understanding (interpreting a natural-language status update). This mirrors the execution-ownership principle already in the growth roadmap.

### n8n — deployed via Docker on the same VPS, Tailscale-only, no reverse proxy (same pattern as Twenty)

**Workflow 1 — Lead creation** (IMAP trigger, fires on new mail): reads new CoachMatch lead emails from Gmail (scoped, read-only IMAP access — not broad Workspace access) → parses lead fields → checks for duplicates by attempting to create the Person in Twenty and branching on whether the mutation succeeds or fails on the email-uniqueness constraint → on a genuinely new lead: creates the Person, sends a Telegram notification (unchanged from the old HubSpot-era logic, new CRM target), sends email 1 of the nurture sequence immediately via SMTP, and sets `leadStatus = MESSAGE_SENT` with `emailTouchCount = 1`.

**Workflow 2 — Email nurture, emails 2 and 3** (Schedule Trigger, runs once daily): queries Twenty for everyone still at `leadStatus = MESSAGE_SENT` with `emailTouchCount < 3`, computes ~~days since creation, and sends email 2 at the 24-hour mark or email 3 at 48 hours~~ ***the calendar day of the sequence the lead is on, and sends email 2 on day 1 or email 3 on day 2 (September 2, 2026 — see the note at the end of §8; the hour-based version silently slipped a day for every lead created after 09:00)*** — one email per run per lead, tracked via `emailTouchCount`. The stop condition for the whole sequence is simply `leadStatus` staying at `MESSAGE_SENT`: if it moves to anything else (a reply on any channel, a manual update), the lead silently drops out of both this query and the WhatsApp watchdog below. No separate "unsubscribe" logic was built — one shared status field does the job for every channel.

**SMTP**: Gmail's SMTP (port 465, implicit TLS), same app password as the read-only IMAP credential, sending as `Iván Koch - Triaperformance <coach@triaperformance.com>`.

**Lead magnets**: the three existing PDF guides (nutrition, mental prep, training zones) were moved off HubSpot's file manager onto the website itself, at `triaperformance.com/guias/` — served by the same Caddy setup as the rest of the site, deployed through the same git pipeline. *Update, July 29, 2026 — path changed:* the PDFs now live at `/assets/guias/*.pdf` after the Eleventy Phase 5 cleanup, with three explicit Caddy redirects from the old `/guias/<file>.pdf` URLs (which are linked from already-sent emails). The bare `/guias/` path has no index and 404s; the real page is the gated, `noindex` `/members/guias/`. See §16.

### Hermes — the WhatsApp lead-status feedback loop

Unlike the email side, there's no WhatsApp Business API in this build (deliberately ruled out — overkill for the volume here, see growth roadmap guardrails). So the WhatsApp side of follow-up is a **feedback loop**: Hermes surfaces who needs a nudge and gives Iván a one-click way to message them, then listens for Iván's own plain-language report of what happened and writes it back to Twenty. Two scripts, deliberately narrow in scope after several early attempts by Hermes to write this logic itself produced broken or incomplete code (see Problem #7 below) — the scripts are fixed, tested, human-written; Hermes's job is limited to calling them correctly.

- **`~/.hermes/scripts/twenty_followup_check.py`** — runs daily via a Hermes-native cron job ("Twenty Lead Follow-up Watchdog," `~/.hermes/cron/jobs.json`). Queries Twenty for people at `leadStatus = MESSAGE_SENT` ~~whose `lastTouchpoint` is more than 2 days old~~ ***who are due their next message on the 3-consecutive-day schedule, counted from `createdAt` (September 2, 2026 — see the note at the end of this section; measuring from `lastTouchpoint` let the email workflow push this one out to 4+ days)***, builds a pre-filled `wa.me` deep link per person (follow-up message copy baked in), and prints a summary — silent if nobody's due, so there's no empty-message noise. Output is delivered to Iván's Telegram home channel automatically as part of the cron job's normal delivery. *(Update, Aug 12, 2026 — the script now also skips **Brazil and Argentina**. The n8n lead workflow has a `Skip WhatsApp Outreach — BR/AR` filter node so those leads never receive WhatsApp outreach, but they still run the email branch, which sets `leadStatus = MESSAGE_SENT` exactly like everyone else. This script filtered on status alone and never looked at country, so it kept building `wa.me` nudges for BR/AR leads and notifying Iván daily about follow-ups that were never supposed to happen. Fix: the GraphQL query now also fetches `addressCountry`, and a `NO_WHATSAPP_COUNTRIES = {brazil, brasil, argentina}` check runs before the phone/touch-count logic. Those leads produce no nudge and no digest line; they close on the email sequence instead — `emailTouchCount >= 3` plus `LOST_NO_RESPONSE_DAYS` (7) of silence auto-sets `LOST_NO_RESPONSE`, so they don't accumulate in `MESSAGE_SENT` forever. Note this check is deliberately placed **before** the "no phone on file" skip, so a BR/AR lead with no phone still gets closed out. Verified against a stubbed 5-lead fixture (BR/AR at 2 and 3 emails, a Colombian lead due for message 2, a Spanish lead due to be marked lost) — correct routing on all five; ~~not yet observed on a real cron run~~ ***confirmed on real cron runs, August 31, 2026*** — Iván reports many consecutive daily digests with zero BR/AR lines. *Marked here rather than only in `open-loops.md`, per the Aug 17 lesson: between a confident present-tense claim and a struck-through one, the confident one wins, so a superseded status has to be struck where it is stated.*)*
- **`~/.hermes/scripts/update_lead_status.py`** — takes a name and one of the valid `leadStatus` values (`NEW`, `MESSAGE_SENT`, `REPLIED`, `IN_CONVERSATION`, `LOST_NO_RESPONSE`, `LOST_NOT_INTERESTED`, `LOST_PRICE`, `WON_CUSTOMER`) as arguments. *(Note, Aug 6, 2026 — a ninth value, `CHURNED_CUSTOMER`, now exists in Twenty but is **not** in this script's hardcoded list, so Hermes cannot set it. Add it to `automation/update_lead_status.py` when the churn convention is decided.)*, searches active (`MESSAGE_SENT`) leads for a name match, refuses to guess if it finds zero or more than one match, and — on exactly one match — runs the Twenty update mutation and refreshes `lastTouchpoint`.

**Telegram use cases now live:**
- Daily automatic nudge: ~~"Following up needed — no status update in 2+ days: ...~~ ***"Follow-ups due today (day 1 = message 2, day 2 = message 3): [name] · [days] · [WhatsApp link]"*** for each lead due a message, silent otherwise. *(Header reworded September 2, 2026 with the schedule change — it now describes where the lead is in the sequence rather than how stale it is.)*
- Free-text status updates, e.g. *"Fabian replied"* → `REPLIED`, *"mark Gaston as won (customer)"* → `WON_CUSTOMER`, *"mark Aimee as lost, too expensive"* → `LOST_PRICE`. Hermes's only job is mapping the sentence to exactly one of the 8 known enum values and calling the script with the right two arguments — it's instructed to ask for clarification rather than guess if the mapping isn't confident (e.g. "customer" alone, before "won" is added, isn't a valid value and shouldn't be silently mapped to one).


*Update, September 2, 2026 — **the three-touch sequence now runs on three consecutive calendar days (day 0 / day 1 / day 2), and both channels were re-keyed off `createdAt` to make that possible.** Iván's call: a lead gets email 1 + WhatsApp 1 on arrival, email 2 + WhatsApp 2 the next calendar day, email 3 + WhatsApp 3 the day after. Calendar days, not business days — a Friday lead is worked through the weekend.*

*Two separate defects had to be fixed to get there, and the second is the interesting one.*

*1. **Elapsed hours were being used where calendar days were meant.** The nurture workflow sent email 2 at `hoursSinceCreated >= 24` against a 09:00 trigger, so **every lead created after 09:00 missed its window by a few hours and slipped a full day** — a 14:00 lead got email 2 on day 2 and email 3 on day 3. Not a rounding error; a systematic off-by-one for the majority of leads. Now compared as calendar dates in America/Cordoba (UTC-3, fixed offset — Argentina has had no DST since 2009, so no tzdata dependency in either runtime).*

*2. **The two channels were fighting over one field.** The WhatsApp watchdog measured its 2-day window from `lastTouchpoint` — which the email nurture workflow overwrites every time it sends email 2 or 3. So each email push shoved the WhatsApp nudge two more days out: the nominal 2-day gap was **4+ days in real runs**, and worse, the watchdog's server-side filter (`lastTouchpoint: { lt: now - 2 days }`) meant a lead emailed that morning was **excluded from the query outright**. Neither script was wrong on its own reading; the coupling was invisible in both. **Standing lesson: a field written by one automation must not be the scheduling clock for another.** `createdAt` is now the clock for both — it is immutable, so the channels cannot move each other — and `lastTouchpoint` is read only by the auto-LOST check, where "when did we last touch this person at all" is genuinely the right question.*

*Both now compute the message that is **due today** (`min(days_elapsed + 1, 3)`) rather than asking whether enough time has passed. That is what makes a second run on the same day a no-op and a missed day resume at the next message instead of skipping one. `LOST_NO_RESPONSE_DAYS` stays at **7** — Iván's call — so a lead now closes around day 9 instead of day 11. The watchdog's server-side date filter is gone; it queries `MESSAGE_SENT` with `first: 200` and decides due-ness per lead. Verified before deploy against stubbed fixtures in both runtimes: 9 Python cases (including a lead emailed the same morning — the case the old filter dropped — a stalled lead self-healing, BR/AR exclusion, and both LOST paths) and 6 Node cases (including a 23:30 lead due the next morning). **Not yet observed on a real cron run** — first live pass is the next lead in.*

Twenty's GraphQL introspection is disabled on this instance, so neither Hermes nor n8n can discover schema/enum values programmatically — the valid `leadStatus` values above were confirmed directly by Iván from the Twenty UI and hardcoded into the script rather than guessed.

*Update, August 5, 2026 — n8n patched 2.30.5 → 2.33.4 in response to n8n's bi-weekly security advisory (multiple High-severity CVEs: GraphQL node credential-header leak, MCP node-schema path traversal RCE, JS task runner sandbox escape via prototype pollution, Git node code execution via repo-local config, among others — full list in the advisory email). Minor-version bump only, no breaking changes per n8n semver. Container recreated in place via `docker run` (image `n8nio/n8n:2.33.4`, same env, same `/root/.n8n` bind mount, same Tailscale-only port binding `100.70.89.17:5678`), preceded by a tarball backup of `/root/.n8n` to `~/n8n-backup-20260805.tar.gz`. Verified live: all workflows (CoachMatch lead creation/nurture, contact-form pipeline, subscription-lifecycle, content-engine publish) still Active post-upgrade, and a real website form submission confirmed end-to-end.

*Update, August 20, 2026 — **the n8n container's run configuration is now written down**, which it never was. §18 and §536 both flag that a container started with a bare `docker run` records its provenance nowhere; this one is that case, and the cost lands at upgrade time, when the run line has to be reconstructed from memory. Captured from `docker inspect` on the live container so it is evidence, not recollection:*

- **Image** `n8nio/n8n:<version>` · **restart** `unless-stopped` · **port** `100.70.89.17:5678:5678` (Tailscale only — the admin UI is not reachable from the internet; only `/api/*` reaches n8n, through Caddy)
- **Bind mount** `/root/.n8n` → **`/home/node/.n8n`**. *The target path is the field that bites: mount it at `/root/.n8n` inside and n8n starts clean, with zero workflows and no error.*
- **Env actually set (9):** `N8N_HOST=100.70.89.17`, `N8N_PORT=5678`, `N8N_PROTOCOL=http`, `N8N_SECURE_COOKIE=false`, `WEBHOOK_URL=http://100.70.89.17:5678/`, `N8N_RELEASE_TYPE=stable`, `N8N_BASIC_AUTH_ACTIVE=true`, `N8N_BASIC_AUTH_USER=admin`, `N8N_BASIC_AUTH_PASSWORD=<Bitwarden>`.
- **Env NOT to pass through** on a recreate, though `docker inspect` lists it: `NODE_VERSION`, `NODE_PATH`, `NODE_ENV`, `PATH`, `SHELL`, `NPM_CONFIG_UPDATE_NOTIFIER`. Those are baked into the image, not configuration — carrying `NODE_VERSION=24.18.0-r0` forward would assert a runtime the new image does not ship.
- **No `DB_TYPE`** → SQLite at `/root/.n8n/database.sqlite`. Back up with the container **stopped**, or the copy may be mid-write.
- **No `N8N_ENCRYPTION_KEY`** → n8n generated one and stores it in `/root/.n8n/config`. **That file is the credentials.** It is inside the bind mount and therefore inside the tarball; lose it and every stored credential is unrecoverable regardless of what else survives.

*Two things flagged the same day and not yet resolved: (1) **`N8N_BASIC_AUTH_*` is probably a no-op on 2.x** — n8n moved to user management in the 1.x line — in which case what actually gates the UI is the n8n owner account plus the Tailscale binding, and the password in that env var is protecting nothing. Confirm before treating it as a control. (2) **No `TZ`/`GENERIC_TIMEZONE` is set, so the container runs UTC**, while Iván is UTC-3. Every `new Date().toISOString().slice(0,10)` in a Code node therefore rolls over at 21:00 local — a Private coaching signup after 9pm is stamped with tomorrow's `signUpDate`. Same class as the RFC-2822 date bug fixed in the CoachMatch parser that morning, found the same way: by reading the real runtime rather than the code.*

## 9. Analytics pipeline — plan-view tracking, GA4, Search Console & site-verification tools (live as of July 21, 2026)

**Site instrumentation.** Google Analytics 4 (property under `coach@triaperformance.com`) was added directly to `website/index.html`'s `<head>` (gtag.js, measurement ID `G-T69KEHW59J`), deployed through the normal git → cron deploy pipeline. Google Search Console was added as a Domain property for `triaperformance.com` — ownership auto-verified because the DNS TXT record from the pre-VPS HubSpot era was still in place, no new DNS work needed. GSC and GA4 are linked (Admin → Product Links) for combined search-query reporting inside GA4.

**TrainingPeaks plan-view pixel.** The 1x1 tracking pixel embedded in each TP plan listing posts `plan_id` + `price` to a Cloud Run service (`plan-tracker-bigquery`, project `training-plan-analytics`), which writes to BigQuery (`training-plan-analytics.tracking_data.plan_views`). This project lives under a separate personal account, `ivankoch87@gmail.com` — not the business Workspace account (`coach@triaperformance.com`) everything else in this document runs under. That split matters operationally: any cross-account work (like the GA4 link below) needs an explicit IAM grant.

*Outage, Jun 30–Jul 21, 2026 (fixed):* the project was silently disabled by a Google billing/abuse false positive — 20 days of data lost, full history since June 2025 intact, fixed by linking a billing account. A **$20/month budget alert** (notification only, not a hard cap — a hard cap would recreate this exact outage on purpose) is now scoped to the project. Full incident narrative: `build-log.md` (problem #8).

**GA4 → BigQuery export.** Enabled the same day (Admin → Product Links → BigQuery Links), exporting into the same `training-plan-analytics` project — daily export, not streaming (free tier, sufficient at this volume). Linking required granting `coach@triaperformance.com` the **Owner** role on the GCP project; Editor alone lacks `resourcemanager.projects.setIamPolicy`, which the linking flow needs to grant its own service account BigQuery access.

**New standing infrastructure — analytics Postgres on the VPS, own lane (same separation pattern as Twenty/n8n).** A dedicated `analytics-postgres` Docker container, bound to `127.0.0.1` only (nothing outside the box needs to reach it — no Tailscale exposure needed). Directory `~/.analytics/` (`data/`, `scripts/`, `credentials/`, `logs/`). Table `plan_views` mirrors the BigQuery schema (`event_timestamp`, `plan_id`, `price`, `ip_address`, `user_agent`, `referrer`) with a `UNIQUE(event_timestamp, plan_id, ip_address)` constraint, making repeated syncs safely idempotent. A read-only BigQuery service account (`pixel-sync-vps`, roles: BigQuery Data Viewer + Job User only) backs a Python script, ~~`~/.analytics/scripts/sync_pixel_data.py`~~ **`automation/analytics/sync_pixel_data.py` in the clone** *(path corrected Sep 2, 2026 — the `~/.analytics/scripts/` copy is a pre-§18 leftover that no cron invokes; confirmed by `crontab -l`)*, which pulls everything with `event_timestamp >= MAX(event_timestamp already in Postgres)` and upserts — run nightly at 5am via cron (staggered clear of the existing 2am Twenty backup and 6am website/KB deploy). First run backfilled all 49,105 historical rows (June 2025 to date). A `plan_views_clean` view sits on top, replicating the bot/IP-filtering logic Iván already used in BigQuery (excludes a known non-customer IP and bot/crawler/spider/headless user agents) — the raw table stays untouched for audit, reporting queries use the clean view (21,217 rows after filtering, matching the existing trusted BigQuery query).

Purpose: this VPS copy is now the durable backup of the pixel data, independent of the personal Google Cloud account's billing/closure risk — the exact failure mode that just cost 20 days. It's also the first table in the VPS's analytics warehouse, which as of August 30, 2026 holds pixel hits plus Search Console and GA4 (addendum at the end of this section). Still outside it: sales CSVs and email signups — see `growth-roadmap.md` §Training Plan Storefront).

**Site-verification tools, same day.** Microsoft Clarity (heatmaps + session recordings) added via a manual script tag in `website/index.html`'s `<head>`, alongside the GA4 tag (Clarity project ID `xq3g7pvr9i`). Connected to the GA4 property under Clarity's Settings → Integrations, which lets recordings be filtered by GA4 traffic-source/segment data — worth noting this is a reporting-level correlation only, not a raw-data feed; Clarity's actual recordings and heatmaps live solely inside Clarity's own platform, no BigQuery/Postgres export exists for them. Bing Webmaster Tools and Ahrefs Webmaster Tools were both added via their "Import from Google Search Console" one-click path — no new DNS or file-upload work, both piggyback on the existing GSC domain verification. Same caveat as the GA4/GSC link above: Search Console data itself doesn't flow into GA4's or BigQuery's dataset, it's a reporting-level integration only — a genuine data pull would need the Search Console API separately. *(Still true of the link. The separate API pull was built August 30, 2026 — addendum below.)*

Two items from the same tooling review deliberately not actioned: Rich Results Test (search.google.com/test/rich-results) needs no account and nothing to set up — it's a URL-paste validator to use once plan pages ship with Product schema (Phase 1/2 storefront SEO work, not built yet). Umami (self-hosted analytics) stays parked — only worth adding if GA4's own UI becomes a friction point, per the original call.

*Update, July 31, 2026 — UTM convention for external channel links (distinct from the internal plan→TrainingPeaks UTMs in §17).* `utm_source` = platform (`google`, `facebook`, `linkedin`, `strava`, `trainingpeaks`, `whatsapp`, `email`, `instagram`). `utm_medium` = placement type (`gbp_profile`, `gbp_post`, `bio`, `signature`, `directory`). `utm_campaign` = `profile` for evergreen bio/profile links, or a specific slug for time-bound content (e.g. a GBP post topic/date). `utm_content` = optional variant when a channel needs to split traffic (e.g. running vs. triatlón). Base URL can be the homepage or any specific page (plan page, All-Access landing) — same UTM tail applies regardless of destination. First applied July 31, 2026 across Instagram bio, Google Business Profile (profile + posts), Facebook, LinkedIn, Strava, TrainingPeaks bio, WhatsApp Business, and email signature.

*Update, August 29, 2026 — GSC and GA4 into analytics Postgres: mechanism settled, schema and sync scripts written, handover to Iván. Built but **not yet verified against the real box** — nothing below is "done" until the queries in step 8 return real rows.*

**Why this exists.** Three initiatives were each waiting on the same missing thing and none of them owned it: the content-engine feedback agent (which cannot rank what it cannot measure), the monthly close (GSC is a Tier-M row that `monthly-close-runbook.md` marks *"Not today"*), and the affiliate program (gated on knowing which countries the readers are in). Promoted out of the feedback-agent bullet into its own item in `open-loops.md`.

**The mechanism was an open question and it resolved into two opposite answers, one per source.** The Aug 12 scoping note assumed both halves would use APIs. That is right for one and wrong for the other, and the reason is specific rather than a matter of taste.

- **Search Console → the Search Analytics API, for both the backfill and the nightly feed.** The competing option was Search Console's BigQuery bulk data export. **Verified against Google's own setup doc, not assumed: the bulk export is forward-only** — *"the first export includes data for the day of the export"*, and for anything earlier it tells you to use the API. Choosing it as the sole mechanism forfeits the ~16 months of history GSC already holds. That history is not a nice-to-have here: the feedback agent's stated trigger is *"60–90 days of GSC data"*, and the API satisfies it **on day one for every article published before today** rather than in three months' time.
  ***The hybrid — API to backfill, export to feed — was considered and rejected, which is a change from the Aug 12 assumption.*** It buys unsampled rows and no quota ceiling, neither of which binds at this volume (25,000 rows per request against a ~60-article site). What it costs is two code paths, two failure modes, and a seam at the changeover date where the two sources genuinely disagree — the export nulls anonymized queries and stores `sum_position`, the API omits those rows entirely and returns an average. **A seam like that does not fail loudly; it shows up months later as an unexplained step in a chart.** One path is worth more than the marginal data quality.
  **The export stays the documented escape hatch, and the schema is written in its exact shape** — same column names, same semantics, same alpha-3 country encoding — so switching later is a change of source, not a migration. Trigger to reconsider: a single day's pull needing more than ~4 pagination round-trips, or the daily quota starting to bite.

- **GA4 → the BigQuery export that has been running since July 21, 2026.** The forward-only limitation that argues against the export for GSC **costs nothing here, and this is the finding that decided it: the GA4 property and its BigQuery link were created on the same day** (see the GA4 → BigQuery paragraph above). *The export covers the property's entire life.* There is no history to recover, so the API's one advantage evaporates. Against that, the export needs **no new credential, no new API enablement and no new IAM grant** — it lands in `training-plan-analytics`, the project `sync_pixel_data.py` already reads, where `pixel-sync-vps` already holds BigQuery Data Viewer + Job User. And it is event-level and unsampled, where **the GA4 Data API applies thresholding that silently suppresses rows at low user counts** — precisely the regime this site is in, and precisely the wrong failure mode for a signal meant to drive decisions.

**The account split, resolved per step rather than in general.** The `training-plan-analytics` project (and `pixel-sync-vps`, and all the BigQuery data) lives under the personal account `ivankoch87@gmail.com`; GSC and GA4 live under `coach@triaperformance.com`. The split lands as follows, and only one step touches the awkward side:
- *Email-level grants cross accounts freely.* Adding the service account's address as a Search Console user is done inside GSC under `coach@` and needs nothing from the personal account.
- *Project-level changes are the awkward side, and are already unblocked.* Enabling the Search Console API happens in the GCP project — but `coach@` was granted **Owner** on that project when the GA4 → BigQuery link was made, so this needs no new IAM work. **Confirm that grant still stands before assuming it** (IAM page, step 1 below); it is the one prerequisite the whole GSC half rests on.
- *The GA4 half touches neither.* Nothing to enable, nothing to grant — subject to the one verification in step 2.

**Where this deliberately departs from `sync_pixel_data.py`, and why.** That script pulls incrementally from `MAX(timestamp)` and upserts `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`. Correct for pixel hits, which are immutable events. **Wrong for Search Console, which revises the last several days after first publishing them.** `DO NOTHING` would freeze each day at its first, incomplete version — every row present, every number quietly low, and nothing to see in a log. Both new scripts therefore re-pull a rolling window (GSC 5 days, GA4 3 days) and use `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE`. *Same idempotency guarantee, opposite conflict rule, because the underlying data has the opposite mutability.* Everything else follows the template: one `~/.analytics/.env`, service-account auth, a repo-resident script on a staggered cron.

**Files, all in `automation/analytics/` (repo-resident; nothing hand-edited on the box):**
- `schema_analytics.sql` — DDL, re-runnable.
- `sync_gsc_data.py` — `--backfill` for the 16-month load, no flag for the nightly rolling window, `--start/--end` for a targeted re-pull.
- `sync_ga4_data.py` — same shape. Discovers the `analytics_<property_id>` dataset by pattern, so the numeric property id is never written down anywhere and cannot go stale.
- `country_map.py` — GA4 country **name** → ISO-3166-1 alpha-3. This exists because GA4 emits `"Argentina"` and GSC emits `"ARG"`, and nothing in either feed carries the other's encoding. Unmapped names resolve to `NULL` and are **logged, never guessed** — a wrong country code in the affiliate analysis is worse than a null, because a null is visible.

**Schema — four tables, five views.** `gsc_site_query` (date × query × country × device) and `gsc_url_query` (date × url × query × country × device) mirror the bulk export's two tables exactly. `ga4_page_day` and `ga4_event_day` carry page and event grain, both with country. `analytics_sync_log` records every run.

Two schema decisions are load-bearing:
1. **Position is stored additively, never as an average.** GSC's `position` is impression-weighted; averaging it across rows is the commonest GSC analysis error there is. The tables store `sum_top_position` / `sum_position` — `(position − 1) × impressions`, the bulk export's own field — and average position is derived in the views and nowhere else. *The bug is not prevented by discipline; it is made unrepresentable.*
2. **`analytics_sync_log` exists so that "zero rows today" and "the cron did not fire" are distinguishable.** An agent reading an empty table cannot tell those apart and would treat a dead pipeline as evidence that nothing ranks. That is this pipeline's most expensive possible failure, and it is a five-column table to rule out.

**Geography is a first-class dimension, kept in the grain of every table** *(added Aug 29, 2026 from the affiliate scoping)*. Whether readers are in Argentina, Colombia, Brazil, Mexico, Spain or the US determines which marketplace can be monetised at all, so the country dimension is never aggregated away at write time. `analytics_geo_28d` puts GSC impressions and GA4 sessions side by side per country, `FULL OUTER JOIN`ed — a country can appear in one source and not the other, and an inner join would answer the affiliate question wrong while looking correct.

**The near-miss signal, which is the point of the whole thing.** `gsc_near_miss` (query × country) and `gsc_near_miss_pages` (url × query × country) return rows at average position 5–20 with real impressions — demand Google has already confirmed, on topics already almost competitive. *"Write a better article about the thing you are on page two for"* is an instruction; *"here is what four blogs wrote about last week"* is not. Country stays in the grain here too: position 7 in Argentina and position 7 in Spain are different commercial facts.

**One expected discrepancy, stated now so it is not later filed as a bug.** Rows whose query Google anonymised are omitted from the API entirely, so query-grouped impressions in `gsc_site_query` run **below** the property totals shown in the Search Console UI. The bulk export would not fix this either — it includes the rows but nulls the query. The gap is Google's privacy floor, not a defect in the pull.

### Handover — Iván's steps, in order

*Steps 1–3 are the GSC half. Step 2 also covers GA4. Step 9 folds in the Business Profile API, because it is the same two clicks and doing it separately means doing it twice.*

**1. Confirm `coach@` still has Owner on the project, then enable the API.** Sign in as `coach@triaperformance.com` → https://console.cloud.google.com/iam-admin/iam → **project picker must read `training-plan-analytics`** (it will not be the default project for this account). Confirm `coach@triaperformance.com` is listed with **Owner**. Then → **APIs & Services → Library** → search **"Google Search Console API"** → **Enable**.
*If the Owner grant is gone, stop and say so — everything below the GSC half depends on it, and re-granting it needs the personal account.*

**2. ✅ CONFIRMED August 29, 2026 — this half needs nothing.** *Both checks came back clean on Iván's first look: `coach@triaperformance.com` holds **Owner** on `training-plan-analytics`, and `pixel-sync-vps@training-plan-analytics.iam.gserviceaccount.com` holds **BigQuery Data Viewer + BigQuery Job User** at project level. The GA4 half's only dependency is therefore satisfied, and the Search Console API was enabled the same session.* **Confirm `pixel-sync-vps`'s BigQuery roles are on the PROJECT, not just the `tracking_data` dataset.** Same IAM page → find `pixel-sync-vps@training-plan-analytics.iam.gserviceaccount.com` → it should show **BigQuery Data Viewer** and **BigQuery Job User** as project-level roles. **This is the only thing the GA4 half depends on.** If those roles were instead granted on the dataset, the service account cannot see the GA4 export dataset and `sync_ga4_data.py` will fail with a clear message saying exactly that.

**3. Add the service account as a Search Console user.** https://search.google.com/search-console → property **`triaperformance.com`** (the Domain property) → **Settings → Users and permissions → Add user** → paste `pixel-sync-vps@training-plan-analytics.iam.gserviceaccount.com` → permission **Restricted** (enough to read performance data; Full is not needed). *This is an email-level grant and crosses the account split without any IAM work.*

**4. Install the one missing library on the box.**
```bash
~/.analytics/venv/bin/pip install google-api-python-client
```

**5. Create the tables.** `PG_USER` and the rest are already in `~/.analytics/.env` — read it rather than retyping anything:
```bash
cd ~/.hermes/triaperformance-docs && git pull
set -a; . ~/.analytics/.env; set +a
docker exec -i analytics-postgres psql -U "$PG_USER" -d "$PG_DB" \
  < ~/.hermes/triaperformance-docs/automation/analytics/schema_analytics.sql
```

**6. Run both backfills by hand, once.** GSC will take a few minutes — it walks 480 days, two requests per day.
```bash
~/.analytics/venv/bin/python ~/.hermes/triaperformance-docs/automation/analytics/sync_gsc_data.py --backfill
~/.analytics/venv/bin/python ~/.hermes/triaperformance-docs/automation/analytics/sync_ga4_data.py --backfill
```

**7. Add the cron lines**, staggered clear of the 05:00 pixel sync. `crontab -e`, then — pointing at the repo copy, prefixed with `git pull`, per the standing rule:
```
15 5 * * * cd ~/.hermes/triaperformance-docs && git pull -q && ~/.analytics/venv/bin/python automation/analytics/sync_gsc_data.py >> ~/.analytics/logs/gsc_sync.log 2>&1
30 5 * * * cd ~/.hermes/triaperformance-docs && git pull -q && ~/.analytics/venv/bin/python automation/analytics/sync_ga4_data.py >> ~/.analytics/logs/ga4_sync.log 2>&1
```

**8. Verify, and report the actual output back.** *This is the definition of done. Code that compiles is not done.*
```bash
docker exec -it analytics-postgres psql -U "$PG_USER" -d "$PG_DB"
```
```sql
-- Is the pipeline alive, and what did each source last do?
SELECT * FROM analytics_pipeline_health;

-- How much history actually landed?
SELECT MIN(data_date), MAX(data_date), COUNT(*) FROM gsc_site_query;
SELECT MIN(data_date), MAX(data_date), COUNT(*) FROM ga4_page_day;

-- The signal this was all built for.
SELECT query, country, impressions, clicks, ROUND(avg_position::numeric, 1) AS pos
FROM gsc_near_miss ORDER BY impressions DESC LIMIT 20;

-- Which article is on page two for what.
SELECT url, query, country, impressions, ROUND(avg_position::numeric, 1) AS pos
FROM gsc_near_miss_pages ORDER BY impressions DESC LIMIT 20;

-- Where the readers are — the affiliate question.
SELECT * FROM analytics_geo_28d ORDER BY gsc_impressions DESC LIMIT 15;
```
*An empty `gsc_near_miss` is not necessarily a failure — it can mean nothing ranks in 5–20 yet, which at this age is plausible. `analytics_pipeline_health` and the `MIN/MAX(data_date)` counts are what discriminate. **And the still-open five-minute Search Console → Indexing → Pages check belongs immediately before this**: if the articles are not indexed, this pipeline will correctly return zero, and that zero is a fact about indexing rather than about content.*

**9. Same trip, the Business Profile half** *(from the approved API application, ticket `8-0766000040742` — `open-loops.md` NOW)*. 🚨 ***Corrected the same day this was written.*** *The instruction here originally read "in the same Cloud project", and that is wrong. It was written without knowing a second project existed.* **There are two projects, in two different organizations** *(found Aug 29, 2026, while Iván ran step 1)*: `training-plan-analytics` under **No organization** — the personal-account project holding the pixel data, the GA4 export and `pixel-sync-vps` — and **`Triaperformance-GBP` under the `triaperformance.com` organization**, which by its name was created for this application. **Business Profile API access is granted against a specific GCP project number and cannot be transferred**, so the GBP work goes wherever the application named, and consolidating by moving GBP into the analytics project would forfeit the approval and mean re-applying — the "weeks, not days" lead time this project has already paid once.
  **The approval email does not name the project** *(checked Aug 29, 2026 — it is a generic congratulations with no project ID or number, so it cannot settle this)*. **The definitive test is the quota, not the enablement:** the Business Profile APIs can be *enabled* in any project, but an unapproved project carries a **requests-per-minute quota of 0** and every call fails. In the candidate project go to **APIs & Services → Enabled APIs & services → Google My Business API → Quotas**; a non-zero limit means that project is the approved one. Check `Triaperformance-GBP` first.
  ✅ ***RESOLVED August 30, 2026: the approval is on `Triaperformance-GBP`.*** *Its quota page shows real limits — 600 requests/minute, 250,000/day, and **Insights Requests per minute: 600**, Insights being the metrics endpoint. A non-approved project shows 0.*
  **Enable in that project:** *My Business Account Management API* (account id), *My Business Business Information API* (location id), *Business Profile Performance API* (the metrics). The legacy **Google My Business API (v4)** was already enabled and is what serves **reviews** — the newer APIs do not cover them, and the monthly close wants review counts.
  🚨 ***CORRECTION, verified same day: this cannot use a service account at all.*** *The line here originally said to add `pixel-sync-vps` as a Manager on the Business Profile. **Google's Business Profile API supports OAuth 2.0 only; service accounts are not a supported credential type.** It was flagged as unverified when written, and checking turned it from "probably fine" into "will not work".*
  **So the credential is an OAuth refresh token for `coach@triaperformance.com`, and one setup detail is load-bearing:** ***the OAuth consent screen must be set to `Internal`, not left in `Testing`.*** *A client in Testing status issues refresh tokens that **expire after 7 days** — the cron would run green for a week and then fail silently, which is this box's signature failure mode (§14, the drift-blocked Monday check-in; §18, the dispatcher that broke on its first real run). `Internal` is available because `triaperformance.com` is a Workspace domain, needs no Google verification review, and does not carry the 7-day clock.*
  **It is a second credential, and it goes in the SAME `~/.analytics/.env`** — client id, client secret, refresh token. *Not a second file: the whole point of the one-file rule is that the rotation checklist has one place per box, not one place per integration.*
  ⚠️ **Consequence that follows from the split and should not be discovered later:** if the approval is bound to `Triaperformance-GBP`, `pixel-sync-vps` is the wrong credential — it lives in the other project — so the GBP sync will need **its own service account**, which is a second key on the VPS. *That is a deliberate decision against the one-credential principle, not a detail to absorb quietly, and it lands on the same box whose Postgres password rotation is already a five-place checklist.* **The unverified part, worth reporting back either way: whether Business Profile accepts a service-account address as a manager.** If it refuses, the fallback is an OAuth refresh token for `coach@` — a second credential of a different and worse kind, since a refresh token expires and a key does not. *No GBP sync script was written this session; this step only clears the setup so it is a one-session job later.*

🚨 ***Path corrected August 29, 2026, before Iván ran a single command.*** *Steps 5–7 above originally read* `~/triaperformance-docs`, *which does not exist on the VPS. The clone is at* **`~/.hermes/triaperformance-docs`** *(container path `/opt/data/triaperformance-docs`), kept current by its own 6am `git pull` cron, and it is the path the existing* `sync_pixel_data.py` *crontab line already uses — §18 records this. **The wrong path was written by pattern-matching what a repo clone is usually called rather than by checking what the runtime actually reads** — which is the exact failure §18 and §13 both name, and the third time this repo has logged it.* ***The test, again: a file existing in the repo proves nothing; ask what path the caller reads and confirm it is inside the clone.*** *Confirm with* `crontab -l | grep sync_pixel` *before adding the new lines — the existing pixel line is the ground truth for both the clone path and the venv path.*

🚨 ***Second correction, same evening, found by running it rather than by reading it.*** *`sync_ga4_data.py`'s dataset-discovery query failed on its first real run with* `400 Syntax error: Illegal escape sequence: \\_`. *Cause: the query used* `LIKE 'analytics\\_%'` *to escape LIKE's single-character `_` wildcard, and* ***`\\_` is an illegal escape inside an ordinary BigQuery string literal*** *— it requires a raw string. Both occurrences replaced with* `STARTS_WITH(...)`, *which has no escaping semantics to get wrong; the second query also moved from* `__TABLES_SUMMARY__` *to* `INFORMATION_SCHEMA.TABLES` *for consistency.* **The lesson is about what was and was not verified, and it is worth more than the fix:** *the Postgres DDL in this pass was tested against a real throwaway Postgres 16 and was clean on the first run; the BigQuery SQL was never parsed by BigQuery at all, and it was the half that broke.* ***A pass that verifies one half thoroughly and the other not at all reports as "verified" and is not.*** *Note the* `--backfill` *path (`earliest_export_day`) was still unexercised at the time of the fix — it only runs with that flag.*

✅ ***Backfill result, August 30, 2026 — and it settles the question the mechanism decision was made to answer.*** **Search Console returned data back to `2025-05-06`** — 1,022 site rows and 1,035 url rows across ~16 months, on a Domain property only verified July 21, 2026. *The property was collecting through the HubSpot era off the legacy DNS TXT record; verification exposed the history rather than starting it.* ***This is the whole argument for the API over the bulk export, paid out:*** *the bulk export would have started at day zero and the content-engine feedback agent's stated "60–90 days of GSC data" trigger would have been a wait until roughly November. It is satisfied today, for every article already published.* **GA4 returned `2026-07-21` to date** — exactly the property's creation date, confirming the other half of the reasoning: there was never any GA4 history to lose, so the export was free of the cost that ruled it out for GSC. *Runtime note: 481 days took ~5 minutes, not the ~22 estimated — empty days return almost instantly, so the walk costs what the data costs, not what the calendar costs.*

*Two small corrections from the same run.* **(a)** Four country names the real data produced — Hong Kong, Pakistan, Slovenia, Vietnam — were unmapped and correctly logged rather than guessed; added to `country_map.py` **from evidence, which is the mechanism working as designed.** **(b)** GA4 emits an **empty string** for some rows' country and device, not `NULL`, so `COALESCE(geo.country, '(not set)')` let two different spellings of *unknown* coexist in one column — `''` and `'(not set)'`. *That is this project's one-owner-per-fact rule violated inside a single column, and it would have surfaced later as a geography query returning two unknown buckets.* Now `COALESCE(NULLIF(x, ''), '(not set)')`; the existing `''` rows are deleted and re-derived by re-running the GA4 backfill.

# ✅ ***VERIFIED LIVE, August 30, 2026 — Iván ran the queries on the real box and they returned real rows.*** *Cron: 05:00 pixel → 05:15 GSC → 05:45 GA4. Data present: GSC `2025-05-06`–`2026-08-27` (1,022 site / 1,035 url rows), GA4 `2026-07-21`–`2026-08-29` (358 page / 1,219 event rows). This entry moves from "built" to* ***live***.

**A cron collision was found and fixed during the handover, and it is the kind that never announces itself.** *The GA4 line was originally written for `30 5` —* ***the same minute as the content-engine publish job***, *and both begin with `git pull -q` in the same clone. Concurrent pulls contend on `.git/index.lock` and the loser dies with "Another git process seems to be running"; the symptom would have been a GA4 sync that silently skipped some mornings.* **Moved to `45 5`.** ***Standing rule for this box: before adding a cron line, print the existing minute/hour columns and pick an unoccupied minute*** — `crontab -l | grep -v '^#' | awk '{print $1, $2}' | sort -k2,2n -k1,1n`. *Every job here now `git pull`s the same repo, so the clone is a shared resource and the schedule is the only lock.*

**The first real near-miss result produced a finding worth more than the confirmation.** *Five rows came back, and* ***one of them is not a search query — it is an LLM prompt***: *"can you please produce a 6 week fitness program aimed to improve strength and lose weight which includes cycling swimming and weight training but excludes running", South Africa, position 6.2. Someone typed a chatbot prompt into Google.* **This matters mechanically, not as a curiosity.** *These views are what the research agent will read, and the instruction they encode is "write a better article about the thing you are on page two for." Handed a 30-word prompt, the agent treats it as a topic.* **Fix: a `word_count` column on both near-miss views — deliberately NOT a filter.** *The consumer decides (a real topic runs ~1–6 words) and the view stays complete, because* ***a threshold that silently drops rows turns a signal into a guess about what the data used to contain***. *Verified against a throwaway Postgres 16 on the actual upgrade path — pre-existing views replaced with the column appended — before being handed over.*

**The other finding is commercial and was invisible before tonight: the near-misses skew Portuguese-market.** *`planos de maratona` (PRT, pos 18.4), `ironman 140.6` (BRA, 9.7), `distancias 70.3` (BRA, 11.3) — three of five rows, against a repo that has spent months describing Portuguese as the thin catalogue. Not acted on here; flagged for the affiliate work, which is what the geography dimension was added for.*

### Site eras — read this before interpreting any Search Console row *(added August 30, 2026, on Iván's question)*

**~14 of the 16 months in `gsc_site_query` belong to a website that no longer exists.** Nothing in the data says so, and no single doc carried this timeline before — it was spread across five dated section headings in this file. It is here because it is the thing you need in order to read the analytics correctly, and `open-loops.md` is a list, not a history.

| From | Era | What the GSC rows mean |
|---|---|---|
| 2025-05-06 → 2026-07-16 | **HubSpot site** | ~14 months. Different URLs, different templates, different everything. **Query + country rows are still valid as market demand.** URL and position rows are archaeology — the pages are gone (the `?hsLang=` relics in §16 are from here). |
| 2026-07-16 | VPS hosting live (§7) | Hand-written site on Caddy. |
| 2026-07-29 | **Eleventy cutover (§15)** | The current site's URL structure begins. |
| 2026-07-30 → 08-06 | Plan storefront live (§17) | `/planes/`, `/en/plans/`, `/pt/planos/` and the individual plan pages. **All five of the first near-miss rows are these pages.** |
| 2026-08-04 | **Blog exists (§20)** | Content engine's publish path closed. ***This is the date that matters for the feedback agent: no article is older than this.*** |
| 2026-08-27 | About pages + credentials (§36) | Days old. Any ranking read on these is noise. |

**The two rules that follow from the table.** *(1)* **Never compare a position before 2026-07-16 with one after it** — it is two different websites wearing one domain. *(2)* **Article-level analysis has a hard floor of 2026-08-04**, and the rolling 28-day views hide this by only ever looking at the last four weeks, which is exactly why it is easy to forget. ***A backfill makes the data older. It does not make the pages older.***

### Attribution, self-traffic, and the alert *(second half of August 30, 2026)*

**Attribution — `ga4_traffic_day`, session grain, its own table.** Channel is a property of a *session*; page views are a property of a *page*. Merging them multiplies a session across every page in the visit. Two grains, two tables.
*Three candidate source fields exist and a probe picked between them rather than a guess:* `traffic_source` is **user-level first touch** — a visitor who once arrived direct reads as direct in every later session; `collected_traffic_source` is populated on only `session_start` and `first_visit` (63 of 526 page_views); **`session_traffic_source_last_click` is on 100% of events** and is the one used.
**Channel grouping is derived in a view, and it deliberately does not reproduce GA4's.** *GA4's default grouping keys off recognised mediums — `organic`, `cpc`, `referral`, `email`. This site's UTM convention (July 31, 2026, above in this section) uses `utm_medium` for **placement**: `gbp_profile`, `gbp_post`, `bio`, `lnk_bio`, `plan_listing`, `signature`, `directory`.* ***Google's own grouping would file nearly every deliberately-tagged link on this site under "Unassigned."*** An unrecognised medium resolves to `Other: <medium>` and never to a silent catch-all — the bucket has to name itself.

🚨 **Self-traffic: at least 25%, and 46% once Iván confirmed the Posadas devices — in the only GA4 data that exists.** *Found by asking why Telegram was the #3 channel: 33 sessions at 82% engagement across **exactly one device**. `plan_views_clean` has filtered bots and Iván's own IP out of the pixel data since July; the GA4 tables shipped with no equivalent, and the monthly close was two days away from reading them.*
**Three layers, because no single one covers it:** *(1)* a GA4 internal-traffic rule by IP — **an Active exclude filter does reach BigQuery** (Google: excluded data *"is never processed and will never be available in Google Analytics or BigQuery"*), ***but filters are never retroactive***, so it cannot touch July 21 → today; *(2)* a localhost guard in `analytics.njk` — the dev server had sent **180 real hits** into the production property; *(3)* rule-based exclusion in the pipeline, which is the only layer that reaches the history.
**Rules, not a list of device IDs** — `user_pseudo_id` is a cookie and a hand-kept list is stale the day it is written. Telegram source, any device that ever sent a `localhost` referrer, `traffic_type=internal`, and `utm_source=internal` (the escape hatch for a tester on a network no IP rule reaches). Detected devices persist in `analytics_internal_devices`, because the nightly run only sees three days and a device that hit localhost in July would otherwise stop being recognised. **Raw rows are kept and `_clean` views filter — the `plan_views` / `plan_views_clean` pattern — because an exclusion you cannot inspect is one you cannot debug.**
*The durable answer to all of it is identity, not network: a GA4 user property set at members-area login would flag a tester by who they are across any device. That is the parked "Deeper analytics — GA4 User-ID" item, and this is the argument for it.*

🚨 ***The correctness bug that mattered most, found by simulating the upgrade rather than by running the new code: an upsert cannot REMOVE a row that should no longer exist.*** *Adding `is_internal` to the grain meant the old `is_internal=FALSE` rows sat alongside the new `TRUE` ones — a test showed **78 sessions where the truth was 45**. The general case is worse than the migration: whenever a row's classification changes, or a source revises a day so a combination stops existing, the stale row survives and is added to every total forever.* **Both syncs now DELETE the window and INSERT, in one transaction** *(GSC per day, GA4 per window)*. ***Upsert is the wrong pattern for a re-derived aggregate; it is only right for immutable events, which is exactly what `sync_pixel_data.py` has.*** *`ON CONFLICT` is kept as belt-and-braces but is no longer what makes a re-run correct — the DELETE is.*

**The alert, in `notify.py` rather than in a new job.** *That file already runs daily, already knows how to reach Iván, and already has the nag-on-failure/stay-quiet logic. A second notifier is a second thing to mute.* It reports any source in `analytics_sync_log` that errored, went stale (>30 h), **or never ran at all** — a missing source means that half never fired. Fails soft and never raises: *a broken analytics check must not take the content-engine notifier down with it.* Verified against a fixture carrying all three failure modes plus two healthy sources, which correctly stayed silent.

***Running tally of what this build taught, because the pattern is more useful than any single fix: every defect in this session — five in the pipeline, plus a top-level `return` that would have killed the Clarity tag on every page — was found by RUNNING something. None was found by reading. And the one class that kept recurring was BigQuery SQL, which is the half of this system with no local parser: four syntax errors reached the box. The mitigation adopted late and worth keeping: for anything exploratory, write FLAT SQL with no aggregates and let Python do the grouping — Python can be tested here, BigQuery cannot.***

### Google Business Profile — live, August 30, 2026

**Approval located on `Triaperformance-GBP`** (quotas: 600 req/min, 250k/day, Insights 600/min — a non-approved project reads 0). Four APIs enabled there. **Credential is an OAuth refresh token for `coach@`, not a service account** — GBP supports OAuth 2.0 only — in the same `~/.analytics/.env`. `automation/analytics/sync_gbp_data.py`, cron **06:15** (06:00 was taken by the KB sync; the schedule was *printed and read* before choosing, per the rule the `30 5` collision produced).

**Three pulls: daily metrics, reviews (legacy v4, wrapped in its own try/except and its own log row because v4 is deprecated and may vanish), and monthly search keywords.** *Two API behaviours decided the parsing and both were found by probing rather than by reading docs:*
**(1)** ***A daily metric whose value is zero omits the `value` key entirely*** *— on a business this size that is the common case: exactly one of thirty days had a value on the first probe. `d["value"]` crashes; `d.get("value")` writes NULL where the truth is a measured zero.*
**(2)** ***Search keywords return either an exact `value` OR a `threshold` meaning "fewer than this".*** *The first pull returned one keyword — the brand name — at threshold 15 and no value. Stored in **separate columns** with a CHECK constraint forbidding both, because coercing a threshold into a value invents a number Google explicitly declined to give, and that number would then be summed and reported as if measured.*

**What the six months of data say, stated plainly because it shapes what is worth building next: GBP is a credibility surface, not a traffic channel.** *60–96 views/month, flat; **2–3 website clicks a month**; call clicks, direction requests and conversations are **zero in every month** — which is what a coaching business with no storefront should expect. ~30 clicks a year cannot be leveraged into revenue at any conversion rate.*
**The row that does earn its place is reviews**, and it converts an assertion into a measurement: **9 in July, 1 in March, zero in every other month**, average 5.00, 46 total, ***all 46 replied***. *`monthly-close-runbook.md` argued "review velocity, not review count" and that the process "demonstrably works and demonstrably isn't running" — that is now a pulled monthly series rather than a claim.* **46 also stops being a hand-typed figure** — the one that sat wrong at 45 on the ES homepage for thirteen days.

✅ ***Local post publishing IS available*** *(open since Aug 17: the approval covered the API family, and whether it reached the post endpoint was unverified). `GET .../localPosts` returns 200 with LIVE posts.* 🚨 **And the response exposed two defects in the hand-written posts, both arguments for publishing from the repo instead:** *(a)* `"languageCode": "en"` **on a Spanish post** — Google uses that to decide who sees it; *(b)* the body sells **"los 321 planes"**, and ***321 has never been correct***: the catalogue has been 301, 303, 327, 328, and `gbp-posts-2026-08.md` says 328 in all four of its mentions. **Not a stale figure — an invented one, from re-typing into the GBP composer instead of pasting from the doc.** ***Standing decision, Iván, same day: the count is 328 (source: the TrainingPeaks marketplace profile), the 321 post is not being corrected, and live copy is not to be audited for plan counts. Kept here only as the reason `post_to_gbp.py` emits no typed figures.***

### GBP article publishing — live, August 30, 2026

`automation/analytics/post_to_gbp.py`, cron **07:15 daily**, Spanish only in v1. One local post per run, photo included.

**A QUEUE, not post-at-publish, and the argument is this system's own behaviour:** *the publish drain is bursty — 20 articles went out in one batch the same day. Post-at-publish would have fired 20 local posts at once, and a local post earns most of its impressions in its first days, so 19 would have been thrown away.* **Paced at one every ~2 days, the same 20 become six weeks of presence.**
**The queue is DERIVED, never maintained** — published ES pieces in `content_pieces`, minus the ids in `gbp_posts_sent`, oldest first. *A second "what to post next" list is the competing-list failure this repo has a standing rule against.* **The ledger row is written only after Google returns a post name**, so a failed post simply retries tomorrow.
**Pacing lives in the script, not in cron:** `0 7 */2 * *` drifts at every month boundary and one missed run would halve the cadence for a month; reading the ledger self-heals.
**No new review gate** — post text is the article's own headline and standfirst, already approved when the article was. *Content-engine V2 is about reducing two gates; adding a third to re-approve approved text would be backwards.*

**Photos: the site's own pick, reimplemented rather than reinvented.** *`cardImage` in `.eleventy.js` hashes the slug against the topic pool. That hash is reproduced in Python and* ***verified identical against the JS across seven slugs***, *so the GBP post shows the same photo as the article's blog card and there is still one rule for "which photo belongs to this article".* **The only deviation is Iván's no-repeat requirement**: when the pick equals the previous post's photo, it steps to the next in the pool — simulated over eight consecutive same-topic articles with no repeat. *Files under 10KB are excluded (Google's own minimum, and it caught the 8-byte `nutrition.jpg` placeholder), and the `sourceUrl` is HEAD-checked first because Google fetches it and a 404 fails the whole post — on failure the post still goes out, without the photo.*
**`IMAGES_DIR` is derived from `__file__`, never from `~`** — §18's dispatcher broke on its first live run for exactly that reason.

*Two crontab notes from the same hour, both worth carrying.* **(a) A duplicate `sync_gbp_data.py` line was added at `15 6`** — two identical jobs at the same minute, both `git pull`ing the same clone, which is the `.git/index.lock` collision this same session had already diagnosed and moved GA4 to avoid. ***The rule was written and then broken within the hour; `crontab -l | awk '!seen[$0]++'` is the one-liner that removes it.*** **(b) A suspected `~/analytics` vs `~/.analytics` log-path defect turned out not to exist.** *Pasted crontab lines rendered without the dot while `grep -c '/\.analytics/logs/'` returned 6 and `grep -cE '[^.]analytics/logs/'` returned 0 — both cannot be true of the same text, so the display was wrong and the count was right.* ***Same lesson as the Aug 17 stale-page false alarm: when a live check disagrees, suspect the check first.*** *A machine count beats a pasted line, every time.*

**On credentials, explicitly: nothing new was created.** Both scripts read the existing `~/.analytics/.env` and the existing `pixel-sync-vps` key. **No second credential file, by design** — the analytics Postgres password already lives in five places and its rotation is already a checklist job (`open-loops.md` NOW). A sixth copy would turn that into a six-place job and break something on a cron hours later, with no obvious cause.

### Two write-path defects fixed before the first monthly close *(August 31, 2026)*

*Both were found by the Aug 31 verification pass, not by a failure. Fixed the same day, deliberately **before** close #1 reads any of these tables — a close is the first consumer that will quote these numbers outside this repo, and a wrong number that has been published is a different problem from a wrong number that has not.*

**(a) `gbp_keywords` destroyed a month on an empty response.** *The write path ran `DELETE ... WHERE month = m` **outside** the `if krows:` guard and re-inserted only when the fetch returned rows — so a valid, empty API answer deleted stored history and put nothing back.* **It is the exact inverse of the GA4 upsert bug fixed the day before, in a script touched in the same session**, and the reviews block thirty lines above it already carried the correct instinct in a comment (*"deleting first would briefly empty a table that other things read"*) that nobody applied downward.

**What makes it the expensive kind of bug is not the delete — it is that the delete was invisible.** *The run recorded `ok`, the log printed `keywords: 0 row(s)`, and on a profile doing 60–96 views a month **zero is the honest expected answer**. Success and data loss printed the same line.* Three changes, and the last two matter more than the first:

- **An empty fetch never deletes.** Replacement still happens, but only with something to replace the rows *with*. Within a live month the value only accretes and a completed month does not fall back to zero, so an empty response is never better evidence than what is already stored.
- **The two empty cases are distinguished and logged.** *Empty with no prior rows* is unremarkable and is what Aug 31 actually returned. *Empty **with** prior rows* means either the month has aged out of Google's serving window or the pull is degraded — the stored rows are kept and the month is **named** in `analytics_sync_log.detail`.
- **`status` gains a third value, `partial`.** A run that kept rows because the fetch was empty is neither a success nor a failure, and **collapsing it into either one is how this bug stayed invisible.** `analytics_pipeline_health` is the only place that difference is ever visible.

🚨 ***The second half of (a) was already costing data, and it is the more interesting one: the keyword window was not the daily window, and nobody had noticed they must differ.*** *`start` is `latest − 7 days`.
