That is correct for daily metrics and **wrong for keywords**, because the two sources behave in opposite ways —* **GSC and the GBP daily metrics *revise* recent days**, *so a rolling window is exactly right and the corrections land;* **GBP keywords are *published late***, *appearing only after the month closes.* **A rolling 7-day window stops reaching a month around the 8th, so every month was frozen at whatever partial state it held on the day the window left it, and the real figures published days later were never fetched.** ~~*That is the likeliest explanation for the four months missing entirely from `gbp_search_keywords` (2025-12, 2026-02, 2026-03, 2026-06) — not that Google had nothing, but that nothing went back to ask after it did.*~~ ***Wrong, and disproved the same evening by the fix's own verification run.*** *The 540-day backfill re-fetched every one of those months through the widened window and Google returned* **empty for all of them** *(plus 2025-02 and 2026-08).* **Google genuinely has no keyword data for those months — the hole is real, not an artefact of the window.** *Worth keeping visible rather than deleting: the cause was proposed from a plausible mechanism and stated as «likeliest» before anything had gone back to ask, which is the same error shape as the Gemini alarm withdrawn on Aug 30 — **get the number first.** The window fix is still correct and still necessary; what it bought was not those four months but the ability to* ***know*** *they are empty, and to collect each month once Google publishes it.* The keyword loop now always reaches back to the **first of the previous month**.

***The two fixes are one fix, and the ordering is the point.*** *Re-reading a closed month would have been actively dangerous under the old write path — a late re-fetch returning empty would have erased a month that was previously correct. **The guard is what makes the wider window safe**, and the wider window is what makes the guard worth having. Fixing either alone would have been a smaller improvement than fixing both.* **`sync_pixel_data.py` is the third behaviour in the same repo — immutable events, `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` — which makes three sources with three correct and mutually wrong write patterns.** *The rule generalises: **the mutability of the source picks the write pattern, and "how often does it change" and "when does it arrive" are separate questions.** Revised-in-place wants a rolling re-read; published-late wants a re-read of the closed period; immutable wants neither.*

***And a test, which is the part worth copying.*** `tests/test_gbp_keywords_guard.py` runs with no VPS, no Postgres, no network and no third-party packages — psycopg2/google/requests are stubbed and the connection is a fake that records every statement handed to it. **It carries the pre-fix code as a fixture and asserts that the old version FAILS**, because a test that passes against both versions is not testing anything. *This is `monthly-close-runbook.md` §7's rule — "a script that reports a clean result without having looked is worse than no script" — implemented rather than quoted.* The keyword loop was lifted out of `main()` into `sync_keywords()` for exactly this reason; it was untestable where it sat, as was the window arithmetic, now `keyword_window_start()` and covered at the year and leap-year boundaries. **16 checks, and the one that matters is the last.**

**(b) The GA4 internal-traffic rule has never tagged a single event.** *`analytics_internal_devices` carries no `traffic_type` row at all.* Of the four self-traffic rules, that is **the only one that survives a cookie reset** — the other three (localhost referrer, Telegram source, `utm_source=internal`) all bind to a `user_pseudo_id`, which is a cookie. So the exclusion that produced the 25%→46% correction rests entirely on cookie-bound rules plus two devices Iván confirmed by hand, **which is precisely the "hand-kept list is stale the day it is written" failure this section's own design note rejected.**

***Nothing said so, because nothing counted the rules separately.*** `refresh_internal_devices` logged one aggregate line — *N matched in window, M known in total* — and **a rule that has never matched looks exactly like a rule that had nothing to match.** *Same shape as §20's `content-<agent>.status`: an empty result that reads as a quiet week.* It now logs a cumulative count **per rule**, and prints an explicit warning while `traffic_type` is zero. *A count per rule, printed nightly, is what turns "we assume the filter works" into "the filter has produced zero for 31 consecutive days."*

**The code half is done; the config half is Iván's and is not done.** The GA4 rule must exist and must tag. **Set the data filter to `Testing`, not `Active`** — *Active drops the events from the BigQuery export entirely, and an event that never lands can never be reclassified, whereas a `Testing` filter tags `traffic_type=internal` and lets the pipeline do the exclusion where the history can still be reached.*

**New view: `ga4_selftraffic_month`.** *The `_clean` views remove self-traffic; this one **measures** it, which is a different job and the one close #1 needs.* It reports external, internal and total sessions per month with an `internal_pct`. **Read `external_sessions` as the reportable figure and `internal_pct` as the confidence attached to it** — that is what lets the close state a GA4 number alongside how much of the month it could not see, instead of quoting a clean number as though it were exact. *Sessions come from `ga4_traffic_day` and never `ga4_page_day`: one session has one landing page, so summing there counts sessions, while summing page rows counts a session once per page it touched.*

⚠️ ***A falling `internal_pct` is not good news and should not be read as one.*** *The detection is four narrow rules over a cookie. A month where the excluded share drops sharply is far more likely to mean the rules stopped catching Iván than that he stopped visiting his own site.*

### Verified on the box the same evening — and the first real read of the new view produced the mistake it was built to prevent *(August 31, 2026)*

**The GBP half is confirmed working, and the verification is stronger than a green row.** *`gbp_keywords` recorded* **`ok`, 34 rows written**, *with `detail` naming every empty month — `2025-02, 2025-12, 2026-02, 2026-03, 2026-06, 2026-08`. The 34 reconciles exactly against a per-month count of the table, and* **`ok` rather than `partial` is itself the correct discrimination**: *none of the empty months held prior rows, so nothing was kept and nothing was destroyed. Under the old code this same run would have deleted six months and logged the identical line.*

🚨 ***And the diagnosis it disproved is recorded above rather than deleted.*** *The four "missing" months are genuinely empty at Google. See the strike-through in (a).*

**One live consequence for close #1, and it is a timing rule rather than a defect:** *`2026-08` is empty on Aug 31 because* **GBP publishes a month's keywords after the month closes** *— which is the whole reason the window was widened.* ⚠️ ***Do not quote an August keyword figure from an August 31 pull.*** *The nightly run self-heals it: the window reaches the first of the previous month, so any run from ~Sept 8 onward collects August once Google publishes it.*

🚨 **The GA4 read is the finding, and it is not the one that was expected.** *`ga4_selftraffic_month` returned* **July 59.3% internal, August 40.2%** *— against the Aug 30 figure of "at least 25%, 46% with the Posadas devices". So on the only two months of GA4 data that exist,* ***the majority of July's sessions were Iván's own.*** *Internal sessions rose 48 → 74 in absolute terms while the share fell, which is the reassuring shape: real traffic diluting a roughly constant self-traffic base, not the rules quietly failing.*

⚠️ ***The trap, caught on the first read and worth more than the percentages: the GA4 property was created 2026-07-21, so "July" is ELEVEN DAYS.*** *Read as two months, external sessions went* **33 → 110** *and the site tripled. Read per day, they went* **3.0 → 3.5**. ***Both are correct and only one is true.*** **This is `monthly-close-runbook.md` §1(b) — a snapshot quoted as a rate — arriving through a different door**, *and the row gave no sign, because a partial month and a full one look identical once summed.*

**So the view carries its own denominator rather than a warning.** `days_covered`, `days_in_month`, **`partial_month`** and `external_per_day` were added the same evening: *any rate computed off a `partial_month = true` row is wrong, and the row now says so.* ***Unrepresentable rather than remembered***, the same treatment already given to additive position storage and `analytics_sync_log`. *The view is `DROP`ped and recreated rather than `CREATE OR REPLACE`d, because a changed column list makes replace* ***refuse*** *rather than warn — §20's `SELECT *` lesson, applied preemptively for once instead of after the fact.*

**`traffic_type` remains zero** (`localhost 2 · manual 2 · telegram 1`), as expected — the GA4 admin half is untouched and is the open item. **The nightly warning now prints, which is the working state, not the failed one.**

#### The `partial_month` column flagged something on its first read, and it was not July *(same evening)*

***The column was added to stop July being read as a full month. On its first run it flagged **August** too, and the session that wrote it had predicted a clean `f`.*** `days_covered = 29`, `last_day = 2026-08-29`, **on August 31.**

**The cause is a gap between a constant and its behaviour.** `sync_ga4_data.py` sets `LAG_DAYS = 1` and asks for yesterday — *but the GA4 daily export table frequently has not landed by the 05:45 run*, so **the newest day actually present is typically `today − 2`.** `ROLLING_DAYS = 3` means the next night's run picks it up: **nothing is lost, it simply is not there yet.** *The constant is not wrong so much as it describes the request rather than the result, and only the result is queryable.*

🚨 **What makes this worth its own note is the date it was found.** *The close was scheduled for September 1.* **A September 1 close reads an August missing its last two days, and §8 of the runbook says a dated close file is never edited after the following close — so it would have been frozen understated, permanently, with every row present and every number quietly low.** ***That is the same failure shape as the `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` decision on the GSC side, arriving through the calendar instead of through a write pattern.*** *Logged as a two-clock rule in `monthly-close-runbook.md` §5, with the per-source completion dates.*

**And it corrected this session's own arithmetic in passing.** *The per-day figures quoted an hour earlier — "3.0 → 3.5" — divided August by 31. The view divided by 29 and returned* **3.8**. *A second-order version of the exact error the column exists to prevent, made by the person who added the column, in the message that announced it.* ***The denominator has to come from the data, not from the calendar.***

**`first_day` and `last_day` are now exposed as columns rather than summarised into the flag**, because `partial_month` alone conflates two things that behave oppositely: *July is partial because the property did not exist before the 21st — permanent, and no later run changes it. August was partial because an export had not landed — cured within 48 hours.* **A close that treats those identically either waits forever or freezes an understated month.**


---

### §9 addendum, September 2, 2026 — `Other: (not set)` was direct traffic, and a coverage check that measured the wrong thing

**The defect.** `sync_ga4_data.py`'s `TRAFFIC_SQL` derived `source`/`medium` as `LOWER(COALESCE(NULLIF(x, ''), '(direct)'/'(none)'))`. **GA4 writes the literal string `(not set)` into `session_traffic_source_last_click.manual_campaign` for a session that arrived with no UTM tags** — not NULL, not `''`. So `NULLIF(x, '')` passed it through, the defaults never fired, and `ga4_channel_day`'s Direct arm (`source = '(direct)'`) never matched. *Every direct session in the property's life fell to the terminal `ELSE 'Other: ' || medium`.* **Close #1 reported it as the largest single channel at 44.6%.**

**Fix:** `NULLIF(NULLIF(x, ''), '(not set)')` on both fields. Two lines, in the view's feeder rather than the view.

🚨 **The transferable finding is the probe, not the bug.** *The Aug 29 note in this section recorded: "`manual_campaign` … a probe on Aug 30 2026 showed is populated on 100% of events." **That was true, and it is what caused this.** The column is populated on 100% of events because its unset value is a string.* **An `IS NOT NULL` coverage check on a column whose sentinel is `(not set)` reports the column's existence and presents it as the column's quality.** *A coverage check has to name the sentinels it tests against.* **Third instance of this family in this repo** — after the aggregate log line that cannot separate "no matches" from "never ran" (§9, Aug 31) and `content-<agent>.status` reading an empty result as a quiet week (§20).

**A comparison that settled a design question by confirming no change.** *`cross_channel_campaign` was diffed against `manual_campaign` across the property's whole life:* **they agree on every row except untagged/direct** *(75 sessions where manual reports `(not set)/(not set)` and cross-channel reports `(direct)/(none)`).* ⚠️ ***And `default_channel_group` is actively worse for this site:*** *it labels the deliberate UTM convention —* `trainingpeaks/plan_listing`, `trainingpeaks/bio` — *as **"Unassigned"**, exactly as `ga4_channel_day`'s header comment predicted when it chose to encode the site's own rules instead.* **The design is confirmed by evidence and should not be re-litigated.** *The `manual_campaign` note above is corrected in place in the script, struck rather than deleted, because the false half is what teaches the lesson.*

⚠️ **The repair had a trap worth recording, because the next schema change will meet it again.** *`ga4_traffic_day`'s unique key includes `source` and `medium`.* **Re-syncing with corrected values would have missed the `ON CONFLICT` target and INSERTED duplicates alongside the originals** — *every direct session counted twice, no error, no warning.* *So: `DELETE FROM ga4_traffic_day WHERE source = '(not set)' AND medium = '(not set)'` (108 rows) first, then `--backfill --start 2026-07-21 --end 2026-09-01`, which reaches the property's entire life because the BigQuery export does.* ***Whenever a sync's derivation of a unique-key column changes, the old rows must be deleted before the re-sync — a re-sync alone is an insert, not an update.***

**Verified, not assumed:** *August totals* **196 sessions in Postgres against 196 in BigQuery**, *and the duplicate check returned zero rows.* **August external channels after the fix:** *Direct 55 · Organic Search 34 · Profile / Bio Link 9 · TrainingPeaks Listing 7 · Google Business Profile 7 · Social 4 · Referral 2 = 118, with `Other: (not set)` gone.* *Nothing moved but the direct sessions; every tagged channel was always being read correctly.*

⚠️ **One accepted cost.** *The fix maps every `(not set)` to `(direct)`/`(none)`, including the single August session **GA4 itself** could not attribute (cross-channel also `(not set)`, which GA4 calls "Unassigned"). It now counts as Direct.* **That slightly weakens the view's own "an unmapped value must name itself" rule, at 1 session in 196** — *judged not worth a third branch, revisit only if the truly-unattributable share grows.*

**Same-day addition — `ga4_selftraffic_month` was returning NULL where it means zero.** *`SUM(...) FILTER (...)` returns NULL when nothing matches, so a month with **no internal sessions detected** and a month with **no data at all** printed identically (blank) — in the view built specifically to measure what the reported numbers cannot see.* **Found on the September row** *(`internal_sessions` blank, `internal_pct` 0.0, two days into a month with certain self-traffic).* 🚨 ***Third instance of this family here***, *after `refresh_internal_devices`' single aggregate line and §20's `content-<agent>.status`.* **The rule the three converge on: an empty result must not render as a zero result** — *a zero should be an assertion, and an absent month should be absent.* `COALESCE(..., 0)` *on both session columns;* `internal_pct` *was already coalesced, which is why the percentage read correctly while the count beside it was blank —* ***a partially-applied guard is how this survived the view's own first review.*** **Fixed in `schema_analytics.sql`; the live view still needs the file re-run** (`open-loops.md` NEXT).

**The direct traffic these figures now name is not the same thing as attributed traffic, and the Caddyfile is why.** *The members gate redirects an unauthenticated visitor with* `redir * /members/<lang>/login?next={http.request.orig_uri}`. `orig_uri` **carries the query string into the `next=` value**, *so a UTM-tagged gated link arrives at GA4 as* `page_location = /members/login/?next=%2Fmembers%2F...%3Futm_source%3D...` — **nested and encoded, which GA4 does not parse.** ***Tagging a link to any gated page is therefore inert, not merely ugly: the session records as Direct either way, and the landing page records as the login screen rather than the page that was shared.*** *That is what the August `/members/login/ — 7` direct row is.* **Any future tagging scheme for members links has to put the UTMs as top-level params on the LOGIN url alongside `next`, not on the destination.*

🚨 **What the fix did not do.** *Direct is now **46.6% of external sessions** and the largest named channel. On a site with negligible brand search that is not an answer, it is a second bucket* — **untagged links pasted by hand (WhatsApp strips referrers, and WhatsApp is where this business sells) mixed with self-traffic the cookie-bound rules miss.** ***Its readability depends on the internal-traffic rule, whose check is dated September 4, 2026 in `open-loops.md`.***

## 10. Storefront data pipeline — plan catalog + weekly-breakdown crawl (July 21, 2026)

**Why.** The storefront plan called for loading the plans inventory onto the VPS as Phase 1 step 1 (that brief was retired Aug 8, 2026; the surviving strategy lives in `growth-roadmap.md` §Training Plan Storefront). Separately, Iván noticed TrainingPeaks' own plan pages render a stats table — "Average Weekly Breakdown" (workouts/week, weekly average duration, longest workout, per activity) — that TP calculates from the actual structured workouts and was never in the manually-maintained inventory CSV. Both landed in the same piece of work.

**New database, same container.** Rather than stand up new infrastructure, added a `storefront` database to the existing `analytics-postgres` container (same VPS, same "own lane" container as the pixel-tracking data — see §9), keeping it logically separate from the marketing-analytics `analytics` database on that same instance. Two tables:
- `plans_raw` — the full inventory CSV (`data/training_plans_inventory.csv` in the repo), loaded as an all-text staging table on purpose, since the source data has known issues (see cleanup below) that would break type constraints on a typed load. 407 rows loaded, cleaned to **386** by removing 13 "Not built" placeholder rows (plan_id literally `"Not built"`) and 8 rows with `link = "Expired"` (no overlap between the two groups). Duplicate `plan_id`s (439394, 439396, 439397, 612974 — already known, see storefront brief) were left as-is; no dedup logic applied yet.
- `plan_weekly_breakdown` — tidy format (one row per activity per plan: `plan_id`, `activity_type`, `weekly_count`, `weekly_avg`, `weekly_avg_unit`, `longest_workout`, `longest_workout_unit`). Units are mixed by design (duration `HH:MM:SS` for most activities, meters for swim, occasionally miles) so the value columns are text, not numeric.

**The crawl.** 330 of 407 plans are published with a working link — those were the crawl targets, using `mcp__workspace__web_fetch` against each plan's live TrainingPeaks page (confirmed via manual samples across every sport type — running, cycling, swimming, strength, triathlon, duathlon — that the stats table is server-rendered and parses cleanly with plain fetches, no JS rendering needed). First attempt used 6 parallel subagents and tripped TrainingPeaks' bot/rate-limit detection partway through; a second attempt with 2 parallel agents also hit it, plus an unrelated bash-tool hang. Net result after both waves: **190 of 330 plans successfully captured** (468 activity rows), **10 genuinely dead links** (mostly 404s on ultra-marathon plans, 2 with a literal `"Expired"` link value, 2 more that use non-standard mile-based units the parser doesn't yet handle), ~~**130 plans not yet attempted**~~ — paused deliberately rather than continuing to fight the rate limit; resumable later without redoing the 200 already-attempted plans (`data/plan_weekly_breakdown_errors.csv` has the exact list and reasons). **Finished July 29, 2026 — see update below.**

*A dead-links finding that used to sit here was **deleted August 8, 2026** at Iván's instruction. It was disproven by a full link check on July 27 — every published plan returned OK — but it had been struck through rather than removed, so it kept getting read, re-quoted and re-raised for three weeks afterwards. Struck-through text still reads as text, and a claim restated in order to deny it is still restated. It is not repeated here. **The lesson survives; the finding does not:** a partial crawl's failures are evidence about the crawl, not about the targets. Link status is measured by `automation/check-plan-links.py`, never maintained in prose.*

**Update, July 29, 2026 — crawl finished; source CSV was already deduped, Postgres wasn't.** Two things closed out in the same session:

1. **Weekly-breakdown crawl completed.** Reconciled the crawl target list against the *current* inventory (381 rows, not the original 407 — 13 "not built" + 21 dead/duplicate rows have been dropped since this section was first written) rather than trusting the stale error list verbatim: 323 plans are currently published with a real link, 190 were already captured, leaving 133 to attempt (not the 130 estimated — the reconciliation caught that 434680's now-fixed link and the 2 mile-unit plans, 475339/476791, were still outstanding). All 133 were fetched sequentially via `mcp__workspace__web_fetch` at a ≥2s pace (no parallelism, matching the July 27 link-check's proven pacing) — **zero bot-detection/rate-limit signals the entire run.** Result: **301 of 323 published plans now have breakdown data** (765 activity rows total in `data/plan_weekly_breakdown.csv`), **22 confirmed dead** (`data/plan_weekly_breakdown_errors.csv`, cleaned of stale entries for plan_ids no longer in the catalog at all) — all 22 are race-year-stamped marathon variants (Tokyo/London/Boston/Barcelona/Santiago/Rio/Lima 2025–26 gym-volume series) returning TrainingPeaks' standard "Training Plan Not Found" page, consistent with expired race dates rather than throttling (confirmed by successfully fetching non-race-specific plans interleaved between clusters of these 404s). The mile-based unit gap is now handled: `weekly_avg`/`longest_workout` store the bare number, `weekly_avg_unit`/`longest_workout_unit` store `mi`. 301 crawled + 22 dead = 323 published, fully reconciled, nothing unaccounted for. **Not yet done:** loading these new rows into the live `plan_weekly_breakdown` Postgres table — file-level work only; see handoff.
2. **Finding from the crawl, fully resolved same day:** plan_ids 612974 (Lima Marathon, Spanish) and 612836 (Lima Marathon, Portuguese — see the dedup note below) both returned "Training Plan Not Found" despite `is_published = TRUE` in the inventory. Iván confirmed both are genuinely unpublished (not a TP outage or a crawler issue). Fixed everywhere: `is_published` flipped to `FALSE` for both in `data/training_plans_inventory.csv` (published+real-link count now **321**, was 323), their `data/plan_weekly_breakdown_errors.csv` reason text updated to state the real cause instead of a bare 404, and `plans_raw` on Postgres updated and confirmed via a live `SELECT` — both rows show `is_published = FALSE`. Nothing further needed on these two.

**Dedup status correction, closed out July 29, 2026.** This section previously said the 4 known duplicate `plan_id`s (439394, 439396, 439397, 612974) were "left as-is; no dedup logic applied yet" in `plans_raw`, and that Postgres (loaded July 21) predated the source CSV's July 22 fix (`git` commit `a3833e8`) and would need the fix applied separately. **Live query against `plans_raw` (July 29) shows this was already wrong — no action was actually needed:** the table currently has exactly **381 rows**, matching the corrected CSV precisely, and `612836` (the corrected Portuguese Lima plan_id) is present with the right content. `plans_raw`'s primary key is on an internal `id` column, not `plan_id` — so this wasn't a constraint silently deduping on load; the table must have been reloaded from the already-fixed CSV at some point after July 22 (not documented when or by whom). Net effect: `plans_raw` and the source CSV already agree, zero duplicate `plan_id`s in either, nothing further to run. This section's "386 rows" figure and the "left as-is" claim above were stale as of this check — treat "381 rows, deduped" as the current true state going forward.

**Update, July 29, 2026 — new rows loaded into Postgres, confirmed via psql.** `plan_weekly_breakdown` now has **765 rows / 301 distinct plan_ids** (up from 468/190), verified by a live `COUNT(*)` query, not just file contents. Two real bugs hit and fixed getting there, worth keeping for next time: (1) `LIKE plan_weekly_breakdown INCLUDING ALL` on the staging table pulled in extra columns beyond the CSV's 7, so a column-less `\copy` failed with "missing data for column" — fixed by naming the 7 columns explicitly in both the `\copy` and the subsequent `INSERT ... SELECT`. (2) `plan_weekly_breakdown.csv` had mixed line endings — the pre-existing 473 rows were CRLF, but new rows appended during this session (both a manual 4-row addition and the subagent's 131-plan crawl) used plain LF, and the inconsistency made `\copy` throw "unquoted newline found in data". Fixed by normalizing the whole file to LF only (`sed -i 's/\r$//'`) before reloading — worth checking for on any future append to this file, since it's evidently been CRLF from whatever originally produced it.

**Source files, repo:** `data/training_plans_inventory.csv`, `data/plan_weekly_breakdown.csv`, `data/plan_weekly_breakdown_errors.csv`.

## 11. Website contact form → Twenty + email + Telegram (live as of July 22, 2026)

The last piece of the CoachMatch-pattern lead pipeline (§8) now also covers direct website leads. `website/index.html`'s contact form (name, email, WhatsApp, sport dropdown, goal) posts to same-origin `/api/contact-form`, which Caddy proxies internally to n8n. Full build is documented in `contact-form-pipeline-runbook.md`; short version:

- **Caddy** needed a `rewrite * /webhook/contact-form` inside the `route /api/contact-form` block before the `reverse_proxy` line — n8n always serves webhooks under a `webhook/` prefix internally, so proxying the raw path unchanged 404s at the Express layer even though the proxy itself is working.
- **n8n workflow** (`automation/contact-form-workflow.json`): dedupe check by email → create Person in Twenty → confirmation email → Telegram notification → `leadStatus = MESSAGE_SENT`, feeding into the same daily nurture workflow and Hermes WhatsApp watchdog a CoachMatch lead uses — no separate nurture logic needed.
- **Real Twenty Person schema, confirmed by reading back a live API response** (rather than guessed from the original HubSpot migration audit in `growth-roadmap.md`): the field is `sport` (not `sportPrimary`/`sport__all_`), and there's no `campaign_attribution` field — `leadSource` fills that role, and turned out to also be an enum (confirmed value: `WEBSITE_FORM`). `sport` itself is an enum too: `RUNNING`, `CYCLING`, `SWIMMING`, `TRIATHLON`, `DUATHLON` (all caps).
- **Confirmed enum values (Iván, source of truth, July 2026):**
  - `leadStatus`: `NEW`, `MESSAGE_SENT`, `REPLIED`, `IN_CONVERSATION`, `WON_CUSTOMER`, `LOST_NO_RESPONSE`, `LOST_NOT_INTERESTED`, `LOST_PRICE`, **`CHURNED_CUSTOMER`** *(added by Iván in the Twenty UI, Aug 6, 2026 — now 9 values. **Nothing writes it yet**: the subscription-lifecycle workflow handles a cancellation by setting `churnDate` and deactivating the member token, and leaves `leadStatus` at `WON_CUSTOMER`. So a churned subscriber is currently indistinguishable from an active one by status alone. See the open item below before wiring anything to it.)*
  - `leadSource`: `COACHMATCH`, `WEBSITE_FORM`, `REFERRAL`, `OTHER`, **`PLAN_CATALOG`** *(added by Iván, confirmed present Aug 6, 2026 — used by the plan-catalog capture, see `plan-lead-pipeline-runbook.md`)*. Originally confirmed via a real 400 from the live API (July 24, 2026) to be only `COACHMATCH`, `WEBSITE_FORM`, `REFERRAL`, `OTHER` — the `WEBSITE`/`ALL_ACCESS_SUBSCRIPTION` options Iván intended to add were never actually saved to the field (added somewhere that didn't take, or not saved). Subscription-lifecycle automation uses `OTHER` for new All-Access subscribers, since the TrainingPeaks confirmation email never tells us the real acquisition channel (website, Instagram, guide link, etc.) and `OTHER` is the most honest fit of the four real options. If Iván wants a dedicated value later, it needs to be added in Twenty under Settings → Data Model → Person → `leadSource` → Add option, then confirmed again the same way (a real API call, not assumed). *Update, Aug 8, 2026 — full list re-read off the Data Model screen by Iván: `COACHMATCH`, `WEBSITE_FORM`, `REFERRAL`, `OTHER`, `PLAN_CATALOG`, **`INSTAGRAM`** — six values. Only `INSTAGRAM` is new to this doc; the other five were already recorded correctly. `COACHMATCH` already existing settles the question raised in `athlete-onboarding-flow.md` §3.2 B2 — no new value needs creating for the CoachMatch onboarding branch.*
  - `customerType`: `PLAN_BUYER`, `ALL_ACCESS`, `OPT1_1_COACHING` — this is the field that actually distinguishes customer types from each other, not `leadSource`. Corrected July 25, 2026: previously documented as `1_1_COACHING` (never actually confirmed against a live response) — a real 400 from the backfill script's first live run gave the exact valid list, including the `OPT` prefix on the coaching value.
  - `coachingStartDate` *(new field, created by Iván Aug 8, 2026 — API name confirmed on the Data Model screen, type Date, `YYYY-MM-DD`)*. **This makes three date fields on Person, not two.** It holds the date coaching service actually begins, which is not the signup date: a CoachMatch athlete can choose a future start (TrainingPeaks even suggests coaches push athletes to the last day of the month so the card is charged then and the remainder of the month is free — Iván deliberately doesn't do this, but athletes can still pick a future date). For Private/TP Payments there is no athlete-selectable start, so `coachingStartDate` equals `signUpDate`. See `athlete-onboarding-flow.md` §3.1.
  - **Consumed by `automation/referral-payout-reminder.json`** *(built Aug 26, 2026, not yet deployed)* — a weekly n8n job (Mon 07:00) that pages the full `/rest/people` collection, selects referrals whose $50 gift card is owed, and Telegrams Iván one list. **It reads five fields and writes none**; `referralRewardSentDate` is filled by hand after the card is sent, and that is what removes an athlete from the next run. *Pagination uses `starting_after` + `pageInfo.endCursor`, the shape confirmed live on Aug 26 by a real paged read returning 238 people. **A count guard (`MIN_EXPECTED_PEOPLE`) turns a silently truncated fetch into a loud alert rather than an empty payout list** — a broken page-2 would otherwise be indistinguishable from a quiet week.*
  - **Referral fields** *(created by Iván Aug 26, 2026; **all three confirmed by a real `GET /rest/people` response**, not the Data Model screen)*: **`referredBy`** — relation, *Belongs to one*, Person → Person, exposed as both `referredBy` (object) and `referredById` (FK scalar); **`referrals`** — the reverse field, an array on the referrer; **`referralRewardSentDate`** — Date, `YYYY-MM-DD`, **on the referred athlete's record**, empty = payout owed. Owner of the program's decisions: `referral-program-brief.md`.
    - ***Created backwards on the first attempt and renamed the same hour, which is the note worth keeping.*** The two labels were swapped — the many-to-one field on Person was named `referrals`, so `person.referrals` returned *the one person who referred this athlete*. **The Data Model screen cannot show you this; the API response can**, because the `...Id` suffix marks the FK and therefore the *many* side. `referralsId` on Person was the tell. *Renamed before anything was written to it — a rename after the first write is a migration.*
    - **`leadSource` is not touched when a referral is recorded, deliberately.** `leadSource` answers *how the athlete arrived* (the commission-bearing channel); `referredBy` answers *who sent them*. Both can be true at once — confirmed live Aug 26, 2026 by two athletes with `leadSource: COACHMATCH` and a `referredBy` set. Same standing rule as `zone-magnet-runbook.md`: overwriting `leadSource` destroys attribution permanently, for a signal that belongs in a different field.
    - **`coachingStartDate` is null for every athlete backfilled before Aug 8, 2026** (the field postdates `backfill_existing_customers.py`), so any query keyed on it silently skips the pre-August book. Confirmed live Aug 26. Use `COALESCE(coachingStartDate, signUpDate)` for anything that must cover existing athletes.
  - `excludeFromSequence` — Boolean. *Documented Aug 26, 2026 (it existed, undocumented, and surfaced in a live response).* **Iván: it means "should not receive all-purpose emails."** Nothing uses it today — no sequences, no nurture running — and it is deliberately one flag doing several jobs. *When marketing/notification/transactional need to be distinguished, it becomes several booleans; parked until then.*
  - **Two separate date fields, confirmed July 25, 2026, easy to conflate**: `purchaseDate` is used for training-plan purchases (this is the field the subscription-lifecycle workflow, §12, already writes for All-Access). `signUpDate` is the separate field for subscribers and 1:1 coaching athletes, plain `YYYY-MM-DD` format — this is the one `backfill_existing_customers.py` (§13 addendum below) writes. Confirmed directly by Iván via Twenty's Data Model settings, not guessed.
  - `preferredLanguage`: `SPANISH`, `PORTUGUESE`, and `ENGLISH` all confirmed — `ENGLISH` verified July 24, 2026 via a live English-subscriber test through the subscription-lifecycle workflow (accepted with no 400).
- **n8n's generic "HTTP Header Auth" credential type doesn't prepend `Bearer ` automatically** — cost real debugging time, since the saved value is masked afterward with no way to re-confirm it visually. Worth remembering for any future n8n + Twenty credential setup.
- Confirmed working with a real production form submission on `triaperformance.com`.

## 12. CoachMatch mis-firing fix + subscription-lifecycle automation (July 24, 2026)

- **CoachMatch bug found and fixed**: the `Email Trigger (IMAP)` node in `CoachMatch Lead Automation` had zero filter criteria — it fired on every single email in the inbox, silently creating junk Person records in Twenty (blank fields via the regex parser's silent-empty-string fallback) for anything that wasn't a real CoachMatch lead. Fixed by Iván directly in n8n using a **Filter** node (not IF) checking `subject` contains "Coach Match" — confirmed working for both matching and non-matching test emails. Source archived at `automation/coachmatch-lead-automation.json` (built with an IF node as the first-pass fix; Iván's live version uses Filter instead — functionally equivalent, Filter is simpler for a single pass/drop condition).
- **New workflow: Subscription Lifecycle (New + Churn)** (`automation/subscription-lifecycle-automation.json`), watching a dedicated Gmail label (`TP-Subscriptions`, filtered from `receipt@trainingpeaks.com`, "Skip the Inbox" checked) rather than the main inbox — required specifically to avoid a mailbox race condition with CoachMatch's own IMAP poller (fetching mail flips the IMAP `\Seen` flag server-side regardless of which workflow's poll does it, so two pollers on the same folder can silently starve each other).
  - Parses the real "New subscription confirmation" and "Subscription cancellation" (exact match, not "contains" — collides with "...cancellation scheduled" otherwise) emails from TrainingPeaks.
  - Detects which All-Access product (ES/EN/PT) from the real confirmed product name strings (see `triaperformance-pricing-and-positioning.md`), sets `preferredLanguage` accordingly. Auth itself is language-agnostic; only the members-area *content* will be filtered by this field once built.
  - Upserts the Person in Twenty (dedupe by email), setting `customerType: ALL_ACCESS`, `leadStatus: WON_CUSTOMER`, `signUpDate`/`churnDate`, `planPurchased`.

**Correction, July 26, 2026** — the `Create Person (New Subscriber)` and `Update Person (Existing Subscriber)` nodes were setting `purchaseDate` instead of `signUpDate`. Only caught once the two-fields-mean-different-things distinction was confirmed in §13's backfill work: `purchaseDate` is for training-plan purchases, `signUpDate` is for subscribers and 1:1 coaching — All-Access is the latter. The churn side was already correct (`Mark Churned in Twenty` sets `churnDate` properly, no fix needed there). Fixed in both nodes to `signUpDate: new Date().toISOString().split('T')[0]` (plain `YYYY-MM-DD`, matching the confirmed working format, rather than the full ISO timestamp `purchaseDate` had been using). Known consequence: any All-Access subscriber created or updated through this workflow before this fix has `purchaseDate` wrongly populated and `signUpDate` blank — worth a manual one-time correction in Twenty for the handful of real subscribers affected (All-Access has had 2 subscribers total per the growth roadmap, so this is a small, easy cleanup, not a backfill-script-scale job).

*Update, August 8, 2026 — product classifier added; a real routing bug found and fixed.* Building the 1:1 coaching onboarding flow (`athlete-onboarding-flow.md`) surfaced that **`Filter - Is New Subscription` matched on subject alone**, and All-Access and Private 1:1 coaching share the subject `New subscription confirmation` because both are built in the same TP Payments product builder. `Create Person (New Subscriber)` then hardcoded `customerType: 'ALL_ACCESS'`. So any Private coaching signup ran the full All-Access chain: wrong `customerType`, a members-area token issued, and the All-Access welcome email sent. `detectLanguage()` did set `language_unrecognized: true` — verified against the real product name `Coaching personalizado - Un deporte (running, ciclismo, natación)`, which matches none of the three All-Access patterns — but **nothing branched on that flag**, so it was a notification, not a gate.

**Blast radius: zero.** Audited Aug 8, 2026 — no new subscriptions of any kind since the workflow went live July 24, so no "NO RECONOCIDO" Telegram alerts and no damaged records. Nothing to repair.

**The fix, live and tested Aug 8, 2026** — 8 nodes added (mirrored into `automation/subscription-lifecycle-automation.json`, now 36 nodes):

- **`Classify Product`** (Code, `Run Once for All Items`) sits between `Map Language + Build Twenty Fields` and the rest of the chain, and sets `productType` from **two explicit allowlists** — All-Access (3 patterns) and Private coaching (`1-1 coaching`). Confirmed by Iván Aug 8: exactly one active Private product, `Triaperformance 1-1 Coaching`, $149/mo + $50 startup fee, promocode `NOSTARTUP`.
- **`Route by Product`** (Switch v3.2) → `All-Access` to the existing chain, `Private Coaching` to a Telegram, and a **fallback `Unknown` output that alerts and writes nothing**.
- **CoachMatch is now handled at all.** Its email (`Congratulations, You Have Been Hired by a New Athlete`, from the same `receipt@trainingpeaks.com` and so the same `TP-Subscriptions` label) previously matched neither filter and **fell off the end of the trigger silently** — the likely reason the July 29 signup was done fully by hand. A third filter, `Parse CoachMatch Hire` and a Telegram now cover it.

**The generalizable lesson, worth more than the fix:** the branch had no `UNKNOWN` case, so an unrecognised input didn't stop — it proceeded on an implicit default. Any classifier that can only say *"this"* or *"assume this"* will eventually mislabel something. Both allowlists must be explicit and the fall-through must write nothing and shout.

*Update, same day — the full 1:1 onboarding chain shipped on top of this.* The workflow is now **49 nodes**. Beyond the classifier above, it carries a complete coaching branch: both channels converge into one `Normalize Coaching Athlete` node → dedupe-by-email upsert into Twenty (`customerType: OPT1_1_COACHING`, `leadStatus: WON_CUSTOMER`, `signUpDate`, `coachingStartDate`, `planPurchased`) → members token generated and inserted → **welcome email in ES/EN/PT** with the members password and the intake-form link → Telegram. Full record, including every decision and its reasoning: `athlete-onboarding-flow.md` §3.1–3.4.

Three things worth carrying forward from building it:

- **The update path writes six fields and no more.** `sport`, `athleteLevel`, `phones`, `preferredLanguage`, `addressCountry` and `leadSource` are create-only, because `coachmatch-lead-automation.json` already populates them at *lead* creation — overwriting them at conversion would destroy the qualification data the lead pipeline collected. Create-only for acquisition facts, always-write for conversion facts.
- **`queryReplacement` on the Postgres node splits its input on commas and drops trailing empties.** An empty `preferredLanguage` produced a live `there is no parameter $4`. Fixed with an `'UNKNOWN'` sentinel + `NULLIF`. **Standing rule: free text (error messages, notes, anything user-supplied) must never go through `queryReplacement` — use the node's `Insert` operation, which binds properly.**

  *Update, August 12, 2026 — **the `NULLIF` half of that fix was itself a bug, and it propagated.** `NULLIF($4, 'UNKNOWN')` turns the sentinel into a NULL, and the column it was being written to (`subscriber_tokens.preferred_language`) is `NOT NULL DEFAULT 'SPANISH'`. **A column DEFAULT applies only when the column is ABSENT from the INSERT — never when it is present and NULL**, so the default cannot rescue it: the insert would simply fail, leaving a new coaching athlete with a Twenty record and a welcome email but no members-area token. It had not fired only because a separate cosmetic bug meant the value always arrived as `=UNKNOWN`, which is not equal to `'UNKNOWN'`, so `NULLIF` passed it through untouched. Two bugs cancelling out.*

  ***The corrected pattern is a whitelist, not a sentinel.*** *Both token inserts in `subscription-lifecycle-automation.json` now read `CASE WHEN $4 IN ('SPANISH', 'ENGLISH', 'PORTUGUESE') THEN $4 ELSE 'SPANISH' END`. This is strictly better than the `COALESCE(NULLIF(...), 'SPANISH')` originally proposed: it validates against the three values that actually exist rather than blacklisting one known-bad string, so `UNKNOWN`, `=UNKNOWN`, an empty string, a NULL and any future leaked expression all resolve to the same safe value. It also makes the database agree with `auth_service/app.py`, which already falls back to Spanish for anything it doesn't recognise — previously the column and the code had two different opinions about what an unrecognised language means.*

  *Update, August 12, 2026 (same day, after testing) — **the `=` prefix leaks on real values, not only on the `UNKNOWN` sentinel, and the source is one field.** Confirmed by a two-run live test: with the athlete's Twenty record holding `preferredLanguage: "ENGLISH"`, `subscriber_tokens` received `SPANISH`. The leak is in `Coaching Athlete Upserted` assignment #3 and nowhere else — assignment #4 (`leadSourceExisting`), same node, same syntax, compares clean against `'UNKNOWN'` in the Telegram template while #3 does not. The signature (`=UNKNOWN` in output, expression otherwise evaluating correctly) is a stored value beginning `==`: n8n consumes the first `=` as its expression marker and the second becomes a literal character in the result. **Look at the raw value of that one field, not at the node's rendered preview** — the preview shows the evaluated result and hides the leading `=`.*

  *Two things this makes standing practice. **(1) Test a language/enum field with a real non-default value, never only with its fallback.** The `UNKNOWN` path was tested repeatedly and showed the bug as a cosmetic oddity; one run with a genuine `ENGLISH` turned the same evidence into a routing failure. A fallback value is the one case where "wrong" and "defaulted" look identical. **(2) A strict comparison in a downstream template is a free assertion.** The Telegram node's `=== 'UNKNOWN'` ternary is what proved the value was corrupt, and its sibling field printing correctly is what localised the fault to a single assignment — neither was written as a test, and both did more diagnostic work than the query did.*

  ***Generalised rule, worth more than either instance: a sentinel value only works if you control every producer of that field.*** *Here we didn't — the `=` prefix leak proved it. A whitelist of the values you accept degrades safely when an unexpected producer appears; a blacklist of the one value you expect does not. Note this is the same shape as the `CONTENT_DB_DSN` truncation recorded in §content-engine: a value that is structurally special to the syntax it is being pasted into.*
- **Twenty's duplicate detection considers more than the field you filter on.** Dedupe by email passed, then `createPerson` rejected with `A duplicate entry was detected` because a hand-created athlete existed under the same *name*. With 35 athletes imported by the July 25 backfill this will recur, so `Create Person (Coaching)` uses **On Error → Continue (using error output)** into a dedicated Telegram that names the manual fix. The node's error output is used rather than `neverError: true` deliberately — `neverError` turns a failure into a success-shaped item that flows on until something else breaks, which is the same implicit fall-through as the bug above.

Also shipped alongside: `automation/n8n-error-collector-workflow.json` (Error Trigger → `n8n_errors` table) and `automation/n8n-daily-error-digest-workflow.json` (08:00 email, grouped by workflow, silent on a clean day). Set as the Error Workflow per workflow. The rule chosen: **interrupt for a person, batch for a machine** — a paying athlete stuck without access still gets an immediate Telegram; everything else waits for the morning digest.

~~One thing deliberately **not** changed: `Send Welcome Email` on the All-Access branch remains **hardcoded Spanish**.~~ *Closed August 10, 2026 — rewritten in ES/EN/PT and tested live on all three products.* A new `Build All-Access Welcome` Code node sits between `Insert Token in Postgres` and `Send Welcome Email`; the send node now carries only `{{ $json.to / .subject / .body }}`. Copy and reasoning: `athlete-onboarding-flow.md` §8.

**A worse bug was found in the same node while fixing it.** The body began with the literal line *"NOTE: placeholder copy -- rewrite once the plan-application instructions and guide links are finalized (task: welcome email package)."* It sat inside the `text` parameter after the `=` expression marker, so it **rendered as the first line of the email to the subscriber**. Never delivered — no All-Access signup had come in since this workflow went live on July 24. **A note-to-self written inside a live template is indistinguishable from copy.** Notes belong in a code comment, a sticky note, or a field that isn't sent.

Two smaller corrections made in the same pass:

- **`Telegram Notify New Subscriber` was moved from parallel to sequential.** `Insert Token in Postgres` previously fanned out to *both* the email and the Telegram. The Telegram now reports which language went out, which is a **forward reference** to a node running on the sibling branch — it happens to work under n8n's current branch ordering, and would break silently if that ordering ever changed. Chained: `Insert Token → Build All-Access Welcome → Send Welcome Email → Telegram`.
- **No plan count appears anywhere in the copy.** `open-loops.md` records the catalogue figure moving four times in five days. Templates outlive the numbers written into them.

*Update, July 29, 2026 — checked in Twenty, closed as a non-issue.* All 3 current All-Access customers (roadmap's "2 subscribers" figure was stale) have `signUpDate` correctly populated and `purchaseDate` blank. No corrections needed; the bad-data scenario this section warned about never actually materialized for any real subscriber.

*Update, July 29, 2026 — two more bugs found and fixed in the CoachMatch workflow (`automation/coachmatch-lead-automation.json`) during live testing, unrelated to the mis-firing/subscription-lifecycle items above but same file:*
- **Location-parsing bug.** The parser node (`Code in JavaScript`) assumed the email's `Location:` field always had exactly 3 comma-separated parts (`city, state, country`). Real CoachMatch emails don't always follow that — a lead with just `Location: Brazil` (1 part) put "Brazil" into `city` and left `country` blank; a lead with `Location: Guanajoato, Brazil` (2 parts) put "Brazil" into `state` instead of `country`. Since `country` is what the workflow keys off for `preferredLanguage`, the calling-code/ISO lookup, and `addressCountry`, this one bug cascaded into three visible symptoms: wrong `preferredLanguage` (Spanish instead of Portuguese for Brazilian leads), phone numbers incorrectly flagged as `no_phone: true` despite being valid, and blank `addressCountry` in Twenty. Fixed by re-parsing `Location` so the *last* comma-separated segment is always treated as country, regardless of how many segments exist.
- **WhatsApp-outreach suppression added.** New Filter node `Skip WhatsApp Outreach — BR/AR`, sitting between `If` (success branch) and `Code in JavaScript4`, blocks the outreach Telegram ping (the one with the WhatsApp link "so I reach out") when the lead is from Brazil or Argentina, or when there's no valid phone number at all. The automated pricing email (`Send an Email`) is untouched and still fires for everyone. First build of this filter had a real bug of its own — every `leftValue` was typed as `=={{ ... }}` (double `=`) instead of `={{ ... }}`, which made every condition evaluate as a literal string with a stray leading `=` character glued on, so the Brazil/Argentina checks always read "not equal" and the no-phone check was never actually empty. Fixed to single `=`, retested against Brazil, Argentina, and no-phone mock leads — confirmed blocked in all three cases live.
- Also confirmed, same session: the "raw HubSpot email HTML in Telegram" symptom originally flagged for this workflow was from *before* the July 24 IMAP-filter fix (§12 above) — it was actual mailer-daemon bounces and calendar-notification emails passing through the old zero-filter trigger, not a bug in the current duplicate-lead branch. No code change was needed for that specific complaint; the existing Filter node already covers it.
  - Tested end-to-end against realistic mock emails (via a temporary static Set node feeding the filters directly, since replaying already-read mail doesn't retrigger an IMAP poller that tracks by UID) — full matrix: new subscriber create, existing-subscriber update (routes to `Update Person`, generates a fresh token rather than reusing the old one — every new-sub event issues a new token, current behavior, not yet a deliberate design decision either way), all three languages (ES/EN/PT, `preferredLanguage: ENGLISH` confirmed accepted), churn matched (token(s) correctly flipped to `active = FALSE`) and churn unmatched (fires only the Telegram alert, no Twenty/Postgres writes). All green. Temporary Set node removed, test Person records cleaned out of Twenty, `Members Postgres` credential wired (was `REPLACE_ME`, now set), Gmail label filter confirmed in place, **workflow switched Active July 24, 2026.**

## 13. Members-area auth gate (built and deployed July 24, 2026)

Per-subscriber token system, not a shared password — a churn only revokes the one subscriber's access rather than requiring a rotate-and-rebroadcast to everyone else. Three pieces, all in `automation/members-area/`:

- **`schema.sql`** — new `members` database on the existing `analytics-postgres` container (same "own lane, same container" pattern as the pixel-tracking and storefront data, §9-10). One table, `subscriber_tokens` (twenty_person_id, email, token, preferred_language, active, created_at, revoked_at).
- **`auth_service/`** — tiny Flask service (`app.py` + `requirements.txt` + `Dockerfile`), bound to `127.0.0.1` only, never exposed beyond the VPS itself. Three routes: `GET /members/check` (called by Caddy's `forward_auth` on every `/members/*` request), `POST /members/login` (validates the token from the login form, sets an HttpOnly cookie), `POST /members/logout`.
- **`caddy-snippet.txt`** — the Caddyfile block gating `/members/*` behind the auth-check service, with a `handle_errors` redirect back to the login page on a 401. Flagged as first-pass, needs real testing the same way the `/api/contact-form` rewrite did — Caddy matcher syntax has version quirks.
- **`docker-compose.snippet.yml`** — deploy config for the auth service, port-bound to `127.0.0.1:8091` only.
- **`website/members/login/index.html`** and **`website/members/index.html`** — static login page and a placeholder members home (just confirms the gate works end-to-end; no real content yet).
- **Wired into the subscription-lifecycle workflow**: `Generate Member Token` (random 20-char token, ambiguous-character-free alphabet since it doubles as a human-typed password) → `Insert Token in Postgres` on new subscription, `Revoke Token in Postgres` on churn. Welcome email now includes the real login link + generated password instead of placeholder copy.
- **Access tracking**: `subscriber_tokens` also has `access_count` and `last_accessed_at`, incremented on every `/members/check` call (i.e. every page load inside `/members/`, not just the login event) — answers "how many times has this subscriber actually used it," which a login-only counter wouldn't (the cookie lasts a year, so logins are rare after the first one).
- **Password lookup + resend, both answered directly rather than assumed**: tokens are stored in plain text in `subscriber_tokens` by deliberate design (not hashed) specifically so Iván can query the table and see exactly which password belongs to which subscriber — a real security-adjacent tradeoff, acceptable given what's being protected is training content, not financial/personal data. Resend is now self-service: a new `Webhook - Resend Password` trigger (same `subscription-lifecycle-automation.json` file, independent branch) reached via `POST /members/forgot-password` (Caddy proxies straight to n8n, same rewrite pattern as `/api/contact-form`) looks up the active token by email and re-sends it over the same SMTP credential already used elsewhere — deliberately responds with the same generic message whether or not the email is a real subscriber, standard forgot-password privacy practice.
- **Postgres node schema**: the scaffolded parameter shape for the Postgres nodes held up against Iván's live n8n instance with no rework needed.
- **Resend-password bug found and fixed (July 24, 2026, live testing)**: the `Token Found?` IF node's condition was `{{ $json.length }} > 0` — but at that point `$json` is a single Postgres row object (`{token, preferred_language}`), not an array, so `.length` was always `undefined` and the condition always evaluated false. Net effect: even when a token *was* found, the workflow silently routed to the generic-response branch and skipped `Send Resend Email` — a subscriber would get the "check your inbox" confirmation with no actual email behind it. Fixed by changing the condition to check `{{ $json.token }}` is not empty instead of array length. A second latent bug in the same branch was fixed alongside it: `Look Up Active Token` (Postgres) emits zero output items on a genuine no-match, and n8n nodes don't fire on zero input items by default — meaning a not-found email would leave the webhook hanging indefinitely rather than returning the generic response. Fixed by enabling "Always Output Data" on that node. Both retested live and confirmed: a found email now receives the real password email, a not-found email gets the generic response with no hang and no email sent.
- **Frontend UX fix, same day**: `website/members/forgot-password/index.html` originally reset the form after submit, leaving the email input and button visible underneath the confirmation message — read as "did this do anything?" Fixed to hide both the form and the intro subtitle on success, leaving just the confirmation text and the "volver al login" link.
- **Deployed and live**: `members` database created, Gmail-label filter for `TP-Subscriptions` confirmed in place, `Members Postgres` credential wired in n8n, temporary Set node removed, subscription-lifecycle workflow active. Full resend/login/churn matrix retested end-to-end post-fix, all green.
- **GA4 added to all `/members/` pages, retroactively (July 24, 2026).** The tag was on every public marketing page but missed on the members pages when they were first built — added to all 8 (`login`, home, `forgot-password`, and the first 5 gated tool pages: `zonas`, `tests`, `carga`, `carrera`, `nutricion`, `kettlebell`) in one pass. Standing rule going forward, also added to `triaperformance-project-instructions.md`: every new page gets the GA4 tag at build time, not after.
- **`access_count` / `last_accessed_at`, what they actually mean**: `access_count` is a lifetime cumulative counter per token — it increments on every successful `/members/check` call, i.e. roughly once per page navigation inside `/members/` (via Caddy's `forward_auth`, which runs on every request under that path). It answers "has this subscriber ever engaged, and roughly how much in total" — it does **not** answer "how many distinct days/sessions," and can't be broken out by day/week/month; a value of 7 could be one five-minute binge or seven visits spread over a month, no way to tell from that column alone. `last_accessed_at` only holds the single most recent timestamp, not a history. For real daily/weekly/monthly active-user reporting, GA4 (now live on these pages, see above) is the right tool — its standard "Active users" report already breaks out 1-day/7-day/28-day active users automatically, segmentable by page path (`/members/*` vs. public pages) with no extra setup required, since it already tracks every pageview with a timestamp. The Postgres counter stays useful for its own thing — a fast per-subscriber lookup ("has X ever logged in") without needing to query GA4 — but it's not, and isn't meant to be, a general analytics source.
- **Access model expanded to 1:1 coaching, decision (July 24, 2026)**: members-area access is now also being used as a retention tool for 1:1 coaching athletes, not just All-Access subscribers -- "if they leave, they're also out of the members area." No code change needed for this: the auth gate is purely token-based (`subscriber_tokens.active`), completely decoupled from `customerType`, so granting access to a coaching athlete is just a data operation, not a schema or auth-service change. The one real gap this opens: All-Access churn is auto-detected (TrainingPeaks cancellation email -> automatic token revoke), but there's no equivalent signal for a 1:1 coaching relationship ending -- offboarding a coaching client from the members area is a manual step (see the manual-revoke query in the Appendix) until/unless a real signal for "this coaching client ended" exists somewhere to hook into.
- **Bulk backfill tool for existing customers**: `automation/members-area/backfill_existing_customers.py` (+ `backfill_template.csv`). One-time-use script, stdlib-only, for granting access to customers who predate the automation (both All-Access and 1:1 coaching -- there's no historical subscription email to replay for either category doing this in bulk any other way). Per row: looks up the Person in Twenty by email (dedupes against existing CoachMatch-era records), creates or updates via Twenty's REST API (deliberately not raw SQL against Twenty's own database -- self-hosted Twenty manages its own internal schema/search indexes/relations, and writing to it outside the API risks corrupting that), generates a token, and outputs a single multi-row SQL `INSERT` for `subscriber_tokens` -- it does not write to Postgres itself, and it sends zero emails on purpose (decided explicitly: no bulk email blast to existing customers). Needs Tailscale network access to reach Twenty (`100.70.89.17:3000`), so it has to run from Iván's Mac or via SSH on the VPS, not from a sandboxed environment without tailnet access. Doesn't protect against double-provisioning on a second run -- check `SELECT email FROM subscriber_tokens WHERE active = TRUE` first and exclude anyone already provisioned from the input CSV.
- **Backfill script hardened through real use, July 25, 2026** — full first real run was 35 1:1 coaching athletes from Iván's own ledger (`coaching_backfill_2026-07-25.csv`), not a general contact migration (see growth-roadmap.md distinction). Several real bugs and confirmed-schema corrections came out of actually running it against live Twenty + Postgres, not guessed:
  - `customerType` valid values confirmed via a real 400: `PLAN_BUYER`, `ALL_ACCESS`, `OPT1_1_COACHING` — previously documented (never confirmed) as `1_1_COACHING`, missing the `OPT` prefix.
  - Two separate, easily-conflated date fields confirmed directly by Iván in Twenty's Data Model settings: `purchaseDate` (training-plan purchases, already used by the subscription-lifecycle workflow) vs. `signUpDate` (subscribers and 1:1 coaching, plain `YYYY-MM-DD`) — the backfill script writes `signUpDate`, on both create and update (unlike `sport`/`planPurchased`/`leadSource`, which are only set on create, to avoid overwriting real data on existing records).
  - Added `phones` (composite field, mirroring the confirmed `emails` shape: `primaryPhoneNumber`, `primaryPhoneCallingCode`, `primaryPhoneCountryCode`), `athleteLevel`, and `addressCountry` to the script, all confirmed working against a real test record.
  - **Design decision on phone country resolution, confirmed by Iván**: the calling code embedded in the phone number itself is always authoritative for `primaryPhoneCountryCode` — never inferred from the free-text `addressCountry` field. These two fields deliberately mean different things: `addressCountry` is whatever the athlete self-declared on their onboarding form (origin/nationality), while the phone's own calling code reflects where they actually are / what number they actually use (e.g. Nadine Tischhauser is Latvian but lives in Mexico and uses a Latvian WhatsApp number; Elisa "Aki" Yamazaki is Japanese but lives in Mexico and uses a Mexican number). Initial concern that these were data-entry errors was wrong — confirmed both are correct as entered. `+1` numbers (shared by US/Canada/Puerto Rico/Barbados/etc.) are the one case still resolved via `addressCountry` as a tiebreaker, since the calling code alone is ambiguous there.
  - Real bug: the SQL-generation code built a 5-column `INSERT` (`twenty_person_id, email, token, preferred_language, active`) but only ever wrote 4 values, omitting `active` entirely — silent until actually run against Postgres (`INSERT has more target columns than expressions`). Fixed.
  - Real bug: `TWENTY_API_KEY` handling didn't account for Twenty's "HTTP Header Auth" credential type not auto-prepending `Bearer ` (same gotcha noted in §11) — if the value saved in Bitwarden already includes the `Bearer ` prefix (likely, since n8n's credential needed it typed in manually), the script was double-prefixing and getting a 401. Fixed to detect and handle either case.
  - New reference file: `automation/members-area/OPERATIONS.md` — day-to-day command cheat sheet (create a single new token, query all/one, deactivate, reactivate, count active, find never-logged-in, find duplicate active tokens, hard delete).
  - *Update, July 29, 2026 — Ronald Yesid (`rongoni6@hotmail.com`) checked for duplicate active tokens from this backfill.* Turned out not to be a true duplicate-active case: of his 2 rows, only 1 was ever active at a time. Deactivated the other (`DQ835eyS6BwAjuNgzzmT`, keeping `j5zzGEMHpaTRHxg3k2zA`) for cleanliness; confirmed via psql only one active token remains for that email.
- ***Update, August 10, 2026 — the auth service was living only on the VPS, outside the repo. Fourth confirmed instance of this pattern.*** Found while deploying §22. The live service runs from `/root/.members-auth/` (compose file `docker-compose.yml`, build context `./auth_service`), a directory created July 24 that has no relationship to the repo clone at `/root/.hermes/triaperformance-docs`. The repo copy at `automation/members-area/auth_service/` was a **reference copy, not the source** — editing it changed nothing, and `docker compose up -d --build` would have rebuilt the old code with a successful build and a healthy container. Silent by construction.
  The two copies had not drifted (`diff` showed only the §22 changes; file mtimes were untouched since July 24 15:06), so nothing was lost — but that was luck, not design. This is the same failure the July 31 migration (§18) was supposed to end; the auth service predates it by a week and was simply missed, because a *reference* copy in the repo looks identical to a *source* copy until you check what the build context points at.
  **Fixed the same day** by pointing the compose build context at the repo: `build: /root/.hermes/triaperformance-docs/automation/members-area/auth_service`. The compose file itself deliberately stays on the box and out of git — it carries the real `MEMBERS_DB_DSN` password; the repo keeps `docker-compose.snippet.yml` with `CHANGE_ME`. After this, an auth-service change is push → `docker compose up -d --build`, with no copy step to forget.

  ***Correction, August 12, 2026 — the line above is wrong. The fix did not take.*** *The first run of `automation/audit-runtime-paths.sh` flagged the container, and reading the live compose file settled it: it still says `build: ./auth_service`, relative to `/root/.members-auth/`. **The live auth service is still built from outside the clone**, exactly as it was before August 10, and `automation/members-area/auth_service/app.py` is still a reference copy that changes nothing when edited. Whether the change was never applied or was applied and lost, the record said "fixed" for two days while the runtime disagreed.*
  ***The lesson is not "be more careful."*** *This entry was written the same day as the fix, in good faith, describing an intended end state — and there is no way to tell an intended state from a verified one by reading it later. **A fix to a runtime path is not recorded as done until the runtime has been re-inspected and the inspection output pasted into the note.** For this class specifically that means `grep -n -A4 build: <compose file>` after the rebuild, not the diff of the change. That is now the standard this section is held to, and it is why the audit script exists: `git diff` proves what you edited, never what the box loads.*
  ***Actually fixed August 12, 2026, with the inspection output that the first attempt lacked.*** *`diff` first confirmed the two `app.py` copies were still identical (171 lines each), so the rebuild could not change behaviour — the check the Aug 10 attempt skipped. Then the build context was repointed and rebuilt. Evidence, in the order it was gathered:*

```
$ grep -n -E 'build:|image:' /root/.members-auth/docker-compose.yml
9:    build: /root/.hermes/triaperformance-docs/automation/members-area/auth_service

$ docker ps --filter name=members-auth --format '{{.Names}}  {{.Status}}'
members-auth-members-auth-1  Up 5 seconds

$ curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n' http://127.0.0.1:8091/members/check
401
```

  *The 401 is the correct answer, not a failure: `/members/check` returning 401 to an unauthenticated request is exactly what the Caddy `forward_auth` gate depends on. **`docker inspect … com.docker.compose.service.build.context` returned empty**, which is normal — this compose version does not write that label, and it is why `automation/audit-runtime-paths.sh` was changed the same day to report a labels-only match as CHECK rather than as a finding.*

  ***What this evidence does and does not prove.*** *It proves the compose file points into the clone and that the container was rebuilt after that edit. It does not, by itself, prove the running image was compiled from those bytes — and that distinction is the entire subject of this section, so it should not be glossed. **The free, decisive proof arrives the next time `app.py` genuinely changes:** edit it in the repo, push, let the box pull, `docker compose up -d --build`, and confirm the change is live. Until that has happened once, treat this as strong evidence rather than a closed loop. A green container has already misled this project once, on August 10, and the lesson was not that the container lied — it was that "it is running" answers a different question from "it is running the code I edited."*
  **The generalisable test, worth applying to anything else containerised on this box:** a file existing in the repo proves nothing. Ask what path the *runtime* reads — a compose `build:` context, a crontab line, a Dockerfile `COPY` source — and confirm that path is inside the repo clone. `docker inspect <container> --format '{{index .Config.Labels "com.docker.compose.project.working_dir"}}'` answers it in one command for anything compose-managed.
- **Where "real analytics" goes next (planning note, not yet built)**: three genuinely different data sources answer three different questions, and they shouldn't be conflated into one number: (1) subscriber/customer counts (active All-Access count, churn, tenure) live in Twenty CRM (`customerType`, `purchaseDate`, `churnDate`) — a CRM query/report, not a web-analytics question; (2) aggregate usage (DAU/WAU/MAU, which tool pages get used, session length) is GA4's job, now instrumented; (3) per-subscriber tool usage tied to identity (e.g. "which athlete used the carb calculator, and how often") needs either GA4's User-ID feature (assign the member token/email as a GA4 user ID on login) or extending the current single-counter Postgres approach into a real per-event log table (`member_access_log`: token, page, timestamp — one row per visit instead of one mutable counter per token). Neither built yet. Longer term, this is the same "VPS Postgres as analytics warehouse" thread already sketched in §9/growth-roadmap.md (pixel hits + GSC + sales CSVs + email signups) — The aggregate half of this shipped August 30, 2026 — GA4 and Search Console both land in `analytics-postgres` nightly (§9 addendum). **What is still unbuilt is the per-identity half**: GA4 User-ID, and a `member_access_log` per-event table to replace the single mutable `access_count` counter. The new tables are page- and query-grain, deliberately not person-grain, so they do not answer "which athlete used which tool". Worth revisiting once there's enough member volume for the aggregate numbers to be meaningful.

## 14. Monday coaching check-in automation (built July 26, 2026; ~~not yet validated~~ **validated live August 21, 2026**)

Automates the manual half of "Lunes de feedback" (the weekly 1:1 coaching ritual documented in `methodology.md` §6) — drafting and preparing the weekly WhatsApp message per active athlete — while keeping the actual send manual (one click per athlete). No WhatsApp automation, no bulk sending, nothing contacts an athlete directly without Iván clicking it himself.

**Split across three pieces, same "deterministic script vs. judgment" division used everywhere else in this project (see problem #7):**

- **`automation/coaching-checkin/fetch_active_coaching_roster.py`** (deterministic, human-written) — queries Twenty for `customerType = OPT1_1_COACHING` with `churnDate` blank (decided explicitly *not* to reuse the members-area token's `active` flag for this — two separate concerns, kept separate on purpose), and picks the week's rotating extra-question theme deterministically from `ISO week number % 12` — no state to persist, cycles through the 12-theme list in `monday-message-voice-guide.md` roughly every 3 months.
- **`automation/coaching-checkin/monday-message-voice-guide.md`** — the drafting brief: canonical 3-part structure (feedback on last week / adjustments needed / any novelties) plus the new rotating 4th question, tone notes and real verbatim examples pulled from an actual 10-month WhatsApp thread (Francisco Manon, a real athlete who rarely responds — used specifically because the tone needed to hold steady regardless of response rate), and the 12-theme rotation pool (recovery/sleep, fueling, motivation, life balance, session enjoyment, pain/discomfort awareness — bounded by the same red-line in methodology.md §11, no diagnosing — gear check, confidence/readiness, environment, support system, self-awareness, big-picture goal check).
- **`automation/coaching-checkin/send_monday_checkin_digest.py`** (deterministic, human-written) — takes the roster + Hermes-drafted messages (one complete message per athlete, in their `preferredLanguage`), builds a `wa.me` deep link per person, and emails one HTML digest to `coach@triaperformance.com` — name, drafted message, one-click WhatsApp button, per athlete. Flags anyone missing a phone number rather than silently skipping them.

**Runs via a new Hermes cron job**, Monday 3am server time (`0 3 * * 1`), prompted to: run the fetch script, draft messages per the voice guide, then run the send script. This is a genuine new instance of the "Hermes for judgment, script for mechanics" execution-ownership principle from growth-roadmap.md — the first real day-to-day Hermes cron job beyond the existing CoachMatch WhatsApp watchdog (§8).

**Credentials**: needs `TWENTY_API_KEY` and `SMTP_USER`/`SMTP_PASSWORD` in `~/.hermes/.env`. The SMTP credential is a *separate* Gmail App Password from whatever n8n uses internally (n8n's credential field is write-only, can't be recovered — generating a fresh, independently-revocable one was simpler and safer than trying to extract the old one). Google App Passwords display in space-separated groups of 4 for readability only — stored in `.env` as one unbroken string, no spaces, no quotes (quoting is actually riskier here, since Docker's `--env-file` mechanism doesn't strip quote characters, unlike a shell `source`).

**Known dependency, same as §13**: this reuses the fact that `churnDate` isn't automated for 1:1 coaching yet — accuracy of both the members-area access list and now the Monday roster depends on Iván manually updating `churnDate` in Twenty when a coaching relationship ends.

~~**Not yet validated**: built and unit-tested (link building, HTML generation, theme rotation all confirmed with fake data), but hasn't run against a real Monday yet. First real run should be treated as a dry run — check the drafted messages actually vary from real prior weeks' wording, check the theme feels natural rather than bolted-on, check all `wa.me` links open correctly on click.~~

**Validated, August 21, 2026 — confirmed live by Iván.** In real use every Monday for three consecutive weeks with nothing flagged: drafted messages vary week to week, the rotating theme reads as natural rather than bolted-on, and the `wa.me` links open correctly. Status moves from "built, not yet validated" to **live and operational** — the second standing Hermes cron job alongside the CoachMatch WhatsApp watchdog (§8). Confirmed by Iván's direct statement, not a fresh technical check — same standard the rest of this repo uses for a live metric.

*Update, August 24, 2026 — **the job failed once, silently, exactly the way an unpinned job would.** This job was created July 26 with no explicit model pin, so it ran on whatever Hermes's global inference default was. The Aug 17 migration (§33) moved that default from `gemini-3.5-flash` to `gemini-3.7-flash`; the job's own safety guard then skipped its Aug 24 run rather than execute an unpinned job on a model it wasn't created against ('global inference config drifted since this job was created... unpinned. Skipped to prevent unintended spend.'). The three 'clean' weeks Iván confirmed Aug 21 all predate the migration taking effect — Aug 24 was the first run to actually hit the new default, which is why this surfaced now and not immediately on Aug 17. Output (the failure notice) landed in the Telegram home channel per the normal cron-delivery design — it wasn't invisible, just easy to miss among other Hermes traffic, and there is no status file for this job the way there is for the content-engine crons (`~/.hermes/state/content-<agent>.status`). Fixed by pinning the job to `gemini-3.7-flash` via the Hermes dashboard (`http://100.70.89.17:9119`, cron job management) — same GA/non-rolling-alias reasoning as the global default. Confirmed working same day. **The CoachMatch WhatsApp watchdog (§8) checked and confirmed categorically unaffected — not because it's pinned, but because it runs in Hermes's `no_agent: true` mode**: the scheduler executes `twenty_followup_check.py` directly and relays its stdout verbatim, no LLM call at all, 0 tokens per tick. The model-drift guard only exists for agent-mode jobs (a job Hermes itself drafts against, like this Monday check-in), so a `no_agent` script job is structurally immune, not lucky. **Revised standing lesson: the pin-check only applies to agent-mode Hermes cron jobs** — for anything genuinely mechanical (fetch/transform/notify with no drafting step), `no_agent: true` is strictly safer against this exact failure mode and worth defaulting to whenever a job doesn't need Hermes's judgment. **Standing lesson: any Hermes cron job created before a model migration needs its pin checked (or set) as part of that migration** — §33 pinned the global default and the content-engine env vars but this job was missed, and an unpinned job fails closed (skip) rather than open (silently run on the new model), which is the safer failure but still needs to be caught rather than discovered by a missed Monday.*

## 15. Website build step — Eleventy static site generator (built July 26, 2026; ~~not yet cut over~~ **cut over and live July 29, 2026**)
